---
title: Bloom language
description: Views as functions and modifiers chained in order, every value a Bloom token. The design of @oxy.so/bloom-language, what is built and what is not.
---

# Bloom language

Bloom's UI language is a way to write Bloom interfaces in TypeScript the way
SwiftUI writes them in Swift: a component is a function, and its look is a chain
of modifiers applied in order. Every modifier value is a Bloom token, never a
pixel or a colour; only geometry the view computes as it renders (a size, a
pinned edge, a stacking order, an offset) is a number of points (see
[Styles computed at runtime](#styles-computed-at-runtime)). The package is `packages/bloom-language`
(`@oxy.so/bloom-language`, published from 1.0.0 beside Bloom 23):

```bash
bun add @oxy.so/bloom-language @oxy.so/bloom-data
bun add -d @oxy.so/bloom-language-compiler   # optional: wrap metro.config with withBloomLanguage
```

The language renders through React Native and react-native-web, using Bloom's
existing components and tokens, so a screen written in it looks the same as the
JSX it replaces. A build-time compiler (`packages/bloom-language-compiler`)
optimises it; the runtime stays complete without it. One spec
(`packages/bloom-language/spec/language.json`) defines the vocabulary both use.
On a device the nodes are React Native's native views, and where the platform
has a component of its own the language draws that one: SwiftUI and the system
components on iOS, the lens on Android and OxyOS (see
[Platform components](#platform-components)).

This page is the design: how each part is drawn and why. To learn and use the
language read the guides: [getting started](bloom-language-getting-started.mdx),
[the tour](bloom-language-tour.mdx), [from JSX and NativeWind](bloom-language-from-jsx.mdx)
and [the cheat sheet for agents](bloom-language-for-agents.mdx); the generated
reference lists every [node](bloom-language-nodes.mdx),
[modifier](bloom-language-modifiers.mdx) and [token](bloom-language-tokens.mdx).

## Syntax

```ts
import { view, HStack, VStack, Text, Avatar, Spacer, Action, ActionBar } from '@oxy.so/bloom-language';
import { ReplyIcon } from '@oxy.so/bloom/icons/ReplyIcon';
import { LikeIcon } from '@oxy.so/bloom/icons/LikeIcon';

export const PostRow = view(function PostRow({ post }: { post: Post }) {
  return HStack(
    Avatar(post.author.avatar).size('lg'),
    VStack(
      HStack(
        Text(post.author.name).font('body').weight('semibold'),
        Text(`@${post.author.handle}`).foreground('muted'),
        Spacer(),
        Text(post.time).font('caption-1').foreground('muted'),
      ).spacing('sm'),
      Text(post.text).font('body'),
      ActionBar(
        Action(ReplyIcon, 'Reply').count(post.replies).onPress(reply),
        Action(LikeIcon, 'Like').count(post.likes).active(post.liked).tint('error').onPress(like),
      ),
    ).spacing('xs'),
  )
    .padding('md')
    .spacing('sm')
    .onPress(() => openPost(post.id));
});
```

Differences from the first draft, each forced by the real components:

- `Icon` and `Action` take the icon component (`ReplyIcon`), not a name. A
  name would need a registry that imports every icon, and Metro cannot tree-shake it.
- The colour roles are Bloom's: `error`, not `red`; `muted` is `text-muted-foreground`.
- Avatar sizes are Bloom's avatar rungs (`xs` 20, `sm` 24, `md` 32, `lg` 36), or an app token.
- `count && Text(count)` is a type error: with a count of 0 it would put a bare
  `0` in a view. Write `count ? Text(count) : null`.
- Glass takes SwiftUI's materials: `.glass()`, `.glass(clear)`,
  `.glass(regular.tint('primary'))`, `.glass(clear.tint('primary', 'subtle'))`.
  A tint's transparency is a named token, never a number (see [Glass](#glass)).
- A view takes one props object, as a React component does (`Row({ post })`).

## Node model

A chain produces plain data: a **node** (`kind`, `props`, `children`) plus an
ordered list of **modifiers**. Each modifier is an object with its kind, its
token value and a precomputed cache key (`p:leading:md`). A chain call never
changes the node it is called on. It returns a new node with one more
modifier, so a node can be shared and branched safely.

Children are nodes, `false`, `null`, `undefined` or arrays of those. Strings and
numbers go in `Text`. Each child keeps its position: a child that becomes
`false` leaves a gap, so its later siblings keep their state. An array
(`items.map(...)`) is one position, and its items are keyed by `.id()` or by
index.

## Order means nesting

SwiftUI wraps a new view per modifier. The renderer gets the same meaning from
fewer React Native views. Within one view, the effects stack as padding, then
frame, then background or glass, then border, then corner, then opacity. A chain
that follows that order is one view. A modifier that comes against the order
starts a new view around the previous ones:

- `.padding('md').background('surface')` is one view: the fill covers the padding.
- `.background('surface').padding('md')` is two: the padding sits outside the fill.
- `.corner('lg').background('surface')` is two: a fill after the corner is not rounded.
- A corner sets the radius only; `.clipped()` clips what the view draws to it, as SwiftUI's does (`.corner('lg').clipped()`).
- A second padding on the same edge adds to the first, so it wraps.

Text and icon traits (`foreground`, `font`, `weight`, `leading`, `tracking`) are an
**environment**: set on a stack, they reach every `Text` and `Icon` inside it,
including inside nested views. `grow` and `shrink` place a view in its parent,
so they always go on its outermost box. Press, hover and accessibility props
go there too.

## `view()`

`view(body)` returns a function that makes a node. The body takes one props
object, as a React component does, and runs when React renders the node, as a
React function component of its own, so hooks work in it.

```ts
const Badge = view(function Badge(props: { label: string }) {
  return Text(props.label).font('caption-1').foreground('muted');
});
HStack(Badge({ label: 'New' }), …)
```

- **One fiber.** The component is React's own `memo` of the body's host, with
  no comparator: props compare key by key, `Object.is` each, and the body
  re-runs only when one changes.
- **One theme read, when it paints.** The body is a tree of its own: it reads
  the theme once, where it starts, when a node of it paints from the theme, and
  every node it draws paints from that read (see [Rendering](#rendering)), so a
  theme switch re-runs it once, as it does a JSX component that reads the
  theme. A body that paints nothing from the theme reads none.
- **The environment** (text traits set above the view) arrives through a
  context that is provided only where an environment modifier changed it, and
  is read only when the body takes from it (see
  [What a root costs](#interop)). A view under an unchanged environment adds no
  provider.

A view node takes the universal modifiers. Environment modifiers flow into its
body, and box modifiers wrap it.

**React Compiler.** Expo runs it in `infer` mode, which finds components by JSX
or hook calls. A body that returns chains has neither, so naming the function
is not enough: it needs `"use memo"`. The language compiler adds it to every
function passed to `view()`; without the compiler, write it yourself. A body
that holds manual `useMemo`/`useCallback` still does not compile ("could not
be preserved"), so keep the controller in a custom hook (`usePostRowModel`).

## Environment

A view sets values on everything inside it, as SwiftUI's environment does:
set once on the container, read by every control within.

```ts
VStack(...).disabled(saving)               // every control inside takes no input
Toolbar(...).controlSize('sm')             // every Button, FAB, field… that names no size
HStack(a, b, c).buttonStyle('glass')       // every Button that names no style

export const Compact = defineEnvironment('compact', false);
FeedRow({ post }).environment(Compact, true);
const PostMeta = view(function PostMeta() {
  const compact = useEnvironment(Compact);
  return compact ? null : Text(meta);
});
```

- **Built-in values.** `.disabled()`, `.controlSize()` and `.buttonStyle()` are
  on every view. A node inside takes the value as its own modifier (`disabled`,
  `size`, `style`) when its chain names none, so its own always wins
  (`Button(...).disabled(false)` inside a disabled form). Which nodes take each
  is the spec's (`environment.values`, generated as `ENVIRONMENT_TAKERS`): every
  node with a `disabled`, every node whose size is a control rung, a `Button`'s
  style. `when('disabled', …)` answers an inherited `disabled` too.
- **Where `disabled` reaches.** A stack's `.disabled()`, and any view's that
  takes no `disabled` of its own, reaches everything inside it. A Bloom
  component that takes one (a `ChipRow`, a `CardForm`) keeps it: Bloom
  decides what its content does, as it does for an app's JSX, so a chain that
  disabled one before draws what it drew.
- **An app's values.** `defineEnvironment(name, default)` at module scope; the
  name is the value's identity (a built-in name is rejected).
  `.environment(key, value)` sets it, `useEnvironment(key)` reads it in a
  `view()` body, and `environmentValues.disabled` (`controlSize`,
  `buttonStyle`) reads a built-in one.
- **One mechanism, cheap reads.** Values travel in the same interned
  environment as the text traits: a renderer reads them where it draws a node,
  and across a `view()` boundary `EnvContext` is provided only where a value
  changed, so `useEnvironment` is one context read. A node takes what it
  inherits as modifiers chained before its own, built once per environment and
  node kind.
- **Values are plain.** A value is a token, a string, a number, a boolean,
  `null` or `undefined`, never a raw style or an object; the compiler holds
  `defineEnvironment` to a literal name and default, and a literal
  `.environment(key, value)` to the default's type.

## Custom modifiers

`defineModifier` names a recipe of modifiers, as SwiftUI's `ViewModifier` does,
and `.modifier(m, ...args)` applies it:

```ts
export const sectionCard = defineModifier((v) =>
  v.padding('md').background('card').corner('lg').cornerCurve('smooth').shadow('s'));
export const tag = defineModifier((v, tone: InkToken) =>
  v.padding('horizontal', 'xs').background(tone).corner('full'));

VStack(...).modifier(sectionCard)
Text('Beta').modifier(tag, 'primary/10')
```

- **A pure function of the view.** Nodes are immutable data, so
  `.modifier(m, ...args)` is `m(view, ...args)`: the same chain, the same
  rendering. A recipe chains the universal modifiers (it applies to any view),
  `when()` included; it reads no hooks and stays inside the token system.
- **Compiler.** It checks the recipe where it is defined (each modifier and
  literal token against the spec, as a chain's), and a module-scope recipe is
  the same value at every use, so a chain that applies it is static and hoists
  like the chain it stands for.
- **Where shared ones go.** A recipe several apps repeat belongs in Bloom
  (Bloom-first), not re-spelled in each app.
- **Handed in.** `.modifier(undefined)` applies none, so a view whose part a
  caller may style takes a recipe as a prop (`look?: ViewModifier`) and chains
  `.modifier(look)` as it is (see [Styles computed at runtime](#styles-computed-at-runtime)).

## Styles computed at runtime

Every value is a token, but some of what an app's JSX styles is only known as
it renders: a cell's width from its container, a card's size from its props,
an inset, a role chosen by state, a style a caller hands in. Each kind has one
form in the language, chosen as SwiftUI chooses it, with the rules that hold
everywhere else: no rendered value changes, styles stay static where they can,
and no layout goes to state.

Measured on Mention (the codemod at this commit, `style` kept 222 elements):

| Kind | Elements | The language |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| A static style the codemod did not read: a module constant or `StyleSheet` style held in a list, a choice between two of them (`compact ? styles.a : styles.b`), `StyleSheet.absoluteFill`, `tokens.space.sm` | 44 | Tokens, as any static style: the codemod's to read (below) |
| A static value off Tailwind's scale: an `rgba()` scrim, a radius of half a fixed side, an app constant (`HPAD`), a font size and its line | 36 | Tokens: an app colour, `'full'`, a numeric token; the codemod's to read |
| A theme colour read at render: `useColorTokens()`, `theme.colors.*` in a list or chosen by state, `withAlpha(…)`, a hook that builds the app's ink styles | 73 | A role, chosen at render where the state chooses it: `.background(active ? 'primary' : 'card')`, `'primary/10'` for `withAlpha(primary, 0.1)` |
| A style a caller hands in (`style`, `style?.handle`, `rowStyle`, a spread of props) | 26 | Modifiers, not a style object: the caller chains them on the view it places; a part inside takes a `ViewModifier` |
| Geometry computed at render: a size from props or a measurement, a pinned edge, a stacking order, a nudge, a media's own aspect ratio, a playback fraction | 20 | Points: `.frame({ width: itemSize })`, `.position({ left })`, `.zIndex(count - index)`, `.offset({ y: nudge })`; a ratio is a number, `.aspectRatio(media.aspect)`; a fraction that moves with playback is a `MotionValue` or Bloom's progress |
| Safe-area and bar insets | 11 | `.safeAreaPadding()` for a padding; points for a pinned edge (`.position({ top: insets.top + 8 })`) |
| Spacing computed at render (an indent times a depth, an offset of the header's rows) | 7 | Stays JSX: spacing is a token, chosen at render (`compact ? 'none' : 'md'`), never arithmetic |
| A colour from data (an artwork's accent, a severity's hex) | 4 | A closed set is an app colour table indexed at render (`.background(severity.color[level])`); an open one (an artwork's accent) stays JSX |
| A style of the press state (`({ pressed }) => …`) | 1 | `.when('pressed', (v) => …)` |

No element among them animates a style: Mention's moving views are the
engine's already (`MotionValue`, see [Animations](#animations)). A view drawn
shifted by an amount its props give (a name size's nudge) is an offset of
points, below.

**Points: geometry takes numbers computed at render.** SwiftUI's `.frame`,
`.position`, `.offset` and `.zIndex` take any `CGFloat` and `Double`; so do the
language's, for geometry alone: a frame's six lengths, a pinned view's edges,
its offset and its stacking order take a number of points (`Points`) besides
their tokens.

```ts
const Cell = view(function Cell({ item, size }: { item: Media; size: number }) {
  return Image(item.thumb).frame({ width: size, height: size }).clipped();
});
CheckoutBadge().position({ top: insets.top + 8, right: 'lg' })
Avatar(actor.avatar).zIndex(shown.length - index)
AccountBadge({ kind }).offset({ y: NAME_SIZES[size].nudge })
Embed(<iframe src={url} />).frame({ width: 'full' }).aspectRatio(getPlayerAspect(provider))
```

- **Only what the view computes.** A number written out is a token: `.frame({
  width: 44 })` is a compile error (`'44'`), as a `MotionValue` written out is,
  so a static frame stays one static, hoisted chain. Points are for a value
  the view works out as it renders: a size from a prop, an inset, a column's
  width from its container.
- **Spacing, type and colour stay tokens.** A padding, a margin, a gap, a font
  and a colour take no number: they are the design's scale, and a value
  chosen at render is a token chosen at render.
- **How it renders.** Points are a value like an app token's: the style is
  cached by the number (`#117.5` in its key), so one view drawn at the same
  size again costs a lookup, and on the web a number with no class is inline
  beside the classes. A value that moves every frame (a drag, a scrubber, a
  playback fraction) is a `MotionValue`, which no style caches.
- **Not a layout read back.** A width the app measured with `onLayout` and kept
  in state is still that: the language draws it, but a `Grid`, a fraction of
  the parent (`'1/3'`) or `.aspectRatio()` says the layout without a second
  pass, and is the form for new screens.
- **A ratio is a number.** `.aspectRatio()` is no token: a ratio written out
  (`16 / 9`) and a media's own one computed at render (`media.width /
  media.height`) are both numbers, React Native's `aspectRatio` as it is.
- **The spec.** `points.params` names them (`frame`, `edges`, `zIndex`,
  `padding`, `offset`); the compiler holds every other parameter to tokens and
  rejects a number written out anywhere, with the token to write.

**A style a caller hands in is modifiers.** In SwiftUI a caller modifies the
view it places, and a view never takes a style object. So it is here:

- **On the view itself:** the caller chains it on the node it places,
  `PodcastCard({ podcast }).margin('bottom', 'md')`, and a `view()` node takes
  the universal modifiers around its body (see [`view()`](#view)). A component
  that took `style` only to place its root drops the prop.
- **On a part inside:** the view takes a recipe, `look?: ViewModifier`, and
  chains `.modifier(look)` on that part (`undefined` applies none). The caller
  names the recipe once at module scope, `const handleLook =
  defineModifier((v) => v.padding('horizontal', 'xs'))`, so it is the same
  value on every render and the view's props stay equal.
- **Never a raw style.** A view that passes a style object through to a host
  stays JSX: a style object holds pixels and colours no token names, which
  the language's chains would carry unchecked.

**A colour chosen at render is a role chosen at render.** A theme read
(`useTheme().colors.primary`, `useColorTokens().border`) is that role, and a
condition between two is the condition between two roles, the modifier the
same: `.border(isActive ? 'primary' : 'border', 'thin')`. Bloom's
`withAlpha(colors.primary, 0.1)` is `'primary/10'`. An app's own palette chosen
by a key is an app colour table (`defineTokens({ color: { low: '#64748b',
high: '#ef4444' } })` as `severity`, `.foreground(severity.color[level])`). An
app colour at an opacity is the token's own, as a role's is (`'primary/20'`):
`severity.color[level].opacity('12.5')` is the hex with its alpha in 8 bits
(`#64748b20`), an `rgb()` an `rgba()`, so a table keeps one colour per entry.
The opacities `'12.5'` and `'31.4'` are the hex alphas `20` and `50` (32 and 80
of 255) apps write after a hex.

**Insets.** `.safeAreaPadding()` pads by the device's insets (see
[Layout](#layout)), `.safeAreaPadding(edge, 'content')` by the content's
(`useContentSafeAreaInsets()`); a view pinned below the inset is pinned by
points, `.position({ top: insets.top + 8 })`.

**Spacing computed at render.** A padding the view works out as it renders is
points, the same rule as geometry: a thread's indent times its depth
(`.padding('leading', depth * 12)`), a measured bar and a gap (`.padding('bottom',
barHeight + 20)`). A number written out is still a token (`'44'`).

## Rendering

| Node | Draws |
| --- | --- |
| `HStack` / `VStack` / `ZStack` | React Native `View` (`Pressable` when it takes presses or hovers); a `ZStack`'s children in one box of slots (see [Layout](#layout)) |
| `Spacer` | `View` with `flex: 1` |
| `Text` | Bloom `Text` (or the typography component its `textStyle` names), its ramp font as `variant`. A nested `Text` is a React Native span inside it. |
| `RawText` | React Native's own `Text`: only what its chain names (no Bloom family, size or ink), as a span, so a text that names no family draws the platform face (Roboto on Android, San Francisco on iOS, react-native-web's system stack on the web). What a screen moved from React Native's `Text` draws; new screens use `Text`, which is Inter. |
| `Icon` | the Bloom icon component, at a size rung, filled with the foreground |
| `Avatar` | Bloom `Avatar` (with a presence, `AvatarPresence`) |
| `AvatarGroup` / `Badge` / `IconCircle` / `Loading` | Bloom `AvatarGroup` / `Badge` / `IconCircle` / `Loading` |
| `Skeleton` / `SkeletonGroup` | Bloom's skeleton shapes / `Skeleton.Region` |
| `.pressScale()` / `.hoverHighlight()` | Bloom `PressableScale` as the pressable host / `SubtleHover` as the stack's first child |
| `.pressFade(o?)` | React Native's `TouchableOpacity` as the pressable host, its `activeOpacity` the opacity token |
| `Image` | Bloom `BloomImage` (`@oxy.so/bloom/image`), sized and clipped by its own box (see [Image](#image)) |
| `Button` | Bloom `Button` (`style` is its appearance, `tint` its tone, `shape` is icon-only: `circle` square, `pill` wide, `fit` grows; see [Button](#button)) |
| `ButtonGroup` | Bloom `Button.Group` |
| `Shape` | Bloom `shapes`' `Fill`, `Border` or `Image` (see [Material](#material)) |
| `ActionBar` / `Action` / `ActionGroup` | Bloom `EngagementBar` / `EngagementAction` / `EngagementGroup` |
| `.glass()` | Bloom `Surface` (see [Glass](#glass)) |
| `.blur()` | Bloom `BlurIsland` (`@oxy.so/bloom/blur`; see [Material](#material)) |
| `.corner('concentric')` | Bloom `ConcentricView` (a glass: `Surface radius="concentric"`) |
| `Divider` | Bloom `Divider` (in a menu, the line between rows) |
| `Fill` / `AspectRatio` / `Grid` | Bloom `Fill` / `AspectRatio` / `ResponsiveGrid` |
| `Embed` | the React element as given |
| `Group` | nothing of its own: its children, where it is (React's `Fragment`) |

**Styles are theme-free.** A style resolves once per modifier key into numbers
and colour *roles*, and one cache serves every theme. Each role is painted where
it is cheapest:

- **One theme read per tree.** A tree reads the theme once, where it starts,
  when a node of it paints from the theme: a `toElement` root (beside the
  environment), a `view()` body, and the content of
  a Bloom component that re-themes what it holds (a dialog's, an immersive
  player's slot), which is a tree of its own. Every node of it paints from that
  read where it is drawn: its host takes the palette's colours (built once per
  palette) with no component of its own and no read, as a JSX component paints
  from the theme it read. A drawn node records the palette it was drawn with, so
  a theme switch draws each tree again once, a node that kept its identity and a
  list's rows included.
- **Native:** every role paints that way. A node with live variants is a
  `Conditioned` leaf, which reads what they hold on (the breakpoint, the
  state) and paints from the tree's palette; one that paints the surface fill
  reads that fill alone (`OnSurface`).
- **Web:** a style's role is its canonical CSS variable (`muted` is
  `var(--muted-foreground)`) inside a class (below). Colours that are props,
  not styles (an icon's `fill`, a glass Surface's `fill`, a chip row's
  `fadeColor`, a swatch radio group's options), paint from the tree's palette.

**A node remembers what it rendered.** Rendering a node in the same environment
and slot gives the same element, so a node that keeps its identity across renders
(a module constant, a chain the compiler hoisted, a node the React Compiler kept)
costs one comparison, and React skips its subtree. A fresh node is interpreted.

**Web CSS is generated at build time.** `scripts/generate.mjs` writes one atomic
class per property and token value (about 1,800 today), with declarations from
react-native-web's own style compiler, so a class says exactly what
react-native-web would have written inline. The runtime hands react-native-web
`$$css` class objects for language hosts; a value with no class (an app token's
own number) stays inline beside them, and a Bloom component (Avatar, Surface…),
which reads its style as an object, gets an inline object with roles as CSS
variables. The whole vocabulary has to be in the sheet, not only what sources
name, because a token can be a runtime value (`.spacing(gap)`), so the sheet is
generated from the spec, not by scanning (no PostCSS plugin).

- *Delivery:* the sheet is a constructed stylesheet adopted once per document
  through Bloom's `adoptStyleSheet` (`@oxy.so/bloom/styles`), as Bloom's web
  forks inject theirs (CSP-safe).
- *Order against react-native-web:* react-native-web inserts its sheet at the
  top of `<head>`, and adopted sheets come after every document sheet, so a
  language class beats react-native-web's base `View`/`Text` reset at equal
  specificity. One host never has a language class and a react-native-web class
  for the same property (styleq keeps one per property). Within the language
  sheet, rules follow react-native-web's precedence groups (shorthands such as
  `flex` before longhands such as `flex-shrink`).
- *Logical edges* (`paddingStart`…) are CSS logical properties, resolved by the
  document's direction, which Bloom sets on the root (`useDirectionProps`);
  react-native-web resolves its own against the nearest `dir` prop.
- *Server rendering:* an adopted sheet is not in server HTML, so
  `LanguageStyles` (from the package) goes once around an app that renders on
  a server with Bloom's `ssrStyles`; the router's `layout()` puts it around
  itself (an inner layout writes nothing). It draws its children, then the
  generated sheet and each variant sheet the tree drew, through Bloom's
  `ServerStyleSheet` (`@oxy.so/bloom/styles`), under the ids the renderer
  adopts, so the page is styled before it hydrates and hydration keeps the
  same sheets. Without a server policy, and once hydrated, it writes nothing.

Fonts are Bloom's: a language `Text` is Bloom's `Text`, so it takes Bloom's
families (Inter, the display face for titles), its desktop scale under a
`TypographyScope` and its own ink when no colour is set (see [Text](#text)).
`RawText` is React Native's own `Text` and takes none of them: with no family
of its own it draws the platform face.

## Modifiers

**Colours.** A role is a theme colour on native and its canonical CSS
variable on the web. `mutedFill` is `--muted` (the subtle fill of `bg-muted`;
`muted` stays the quiet ink, `--muted-foreground`), `textTertiary` is Bloom's
`ThemeColors.textTertiary` (on the web `--faint-foreground`, which every preset
holds at `--muted-foreground`'s colour; on Android and iOS the system's tertiary
label where Bloom adapts its colours, so it is not `muted` there), `card` is `--card`,
`destructive` is `--destructive` (Bloom's `ThemeColors.destructive`, which
the engine's error role fills; `error` is the policy's status colour),
`primaryText` is `--primary-text`, `errorText` `--error-text`, `onSurface`
`--surface-foreground`, `accent` `--accent`, and `white`, `black` and
`transparent` are Tailwind's fixed colours (Bloom's `ThemeColors.white`,
`black` and `transparent`, the same in every theme). `surfaceFill` is the
fill of the surface the view sits on, Bloom's `useSurfaceFill()`: the colour a
container around it published (a Screen, a Card, a panel), else its level's,
for chrome that has to be that colour (a tab bar's backing, an avatar's ring
in a skeleton). No theme colour or CSS variable holds it, so the leaf that
paints the view reads it where it is drawn, on the web too (its style is an
object there, its variants tracked in JS, as an app token's are). Only a leaf
whose paint names it reads the surface's context (drawn inside `OnSurface`,
which hands it the fill); every other leaf reads what it did before, so a node
that does not paint it costs what its JSX does. A role at an opacity is one token, `'role/opacity'`,
with an `opacity` token for the amount: `text-muted-foreground/70` is
`.foreground('muted/70')`, `bg-primary/10` `.background('primary/10')`.
Native paints the role's colour at that alpha; the web paints an ink, a
custom property declared in the language's ink sheet as the role mixed with
transparent in sRGB (`--bl-muted-70: color-mix(in srgb, var(--muted-foreground)
70%, transparent)`), exactly the role's colour at that alpha, as Bloom's
`withAlpha` draws it (Tailwind's own mix is in oklab, which paints the same
pixels); a class made from the role's class paints it, so a variant can
override it. An icon's fill is painted at that alpha from the theme.

**Named rungs and numeric tokens.** A scale has two kinds of token, one
mechanism each:

- **Named rungs are the design scale**: Bloom's own (`xs` `sm` `md`…, `tight`
  `snug` `normal`…), held equal to Bloom's tables by `spec.test.ts`. New
  design reaches for these.
- **Numeric tokens are exact values carried over from apps**: the token's
  name is its number, in the unit of its scale, so `'44'` is 44 and cannot
  mean anything else (`spec.test.ts` holds every name equal to its value).
  Space, corner and font size are in points (`'6'`, `'22'`, `'16'`); a line
  height is a named ratio or an exact px height (`'28px'`); tracking is a
  named em amount, an em value (`'-0.02em'`) or px (`'-0.5px'`); an opacity
  is its percent (`'60'` is 0.6, as Tailwind's `opacity-60` and `/60`). They
  are a closed set too: generated from the spec, checked by the compiler, never
  an arbitrary number at a call site. They exist so a codemod moves a screen
  without changing a pixel: Tailwind's `p-1.5` is `.padding('6')`,
  `text-base` `.font('16').leading('normal')`, `text-lg`
  `.font('18').leading('28px')`, `leading-5` `.leading('20px')`.

Which values are numeric tokens: every value the className table found in two
or more places that a request named (space, corner and type steps, the px line
heights, every tracking), and the frame sizes apps use ten times or more
(`'160'`, `'224'`, `'384'`, `'1200'`…). Tailwind's own values are numeric
tokens too where apps use them: its stacking levels `'20'` `'30'` `'50'`, its
shadows (each named by its exact box-shadow), the 3px ring (`'3'`), and the
scale factors apps write (`'95'` `'99'` `'103'` `'105'` `'110'`, in percent).
The same rule takes the leftovers apps write in two or more places: the
stacking levels `'2'` and `'60'` (`z-[2]`, `z-[60]`), the 4px border (`'4'`,
`border-4`), the opacities `'45'` and `'75'`, and the 45° turn (`'45'`,
`rotate-45`), and the dialog and popover widths apps give Bloom's `width`,
`maxWidth` and `minWidth` that the className table also finds in two or more
places: `'240'` `'300'` `'380'` `'400'` `'420'` `'440'` `'480'` `'512'` `'560'`
`'720'`. A line's width has Bloom's own signature stroke besides, `'2.5'`, so a
signature pad's `.strokeWidth()` can name it, and the half-point and
point-and-a-half borders apps write, `'0.5'` and `'1.5'` (`'0.5'` is a fixed
half point on every screen; `hairline` stays the platform's thinnest line). The
sizes Mention writes outside the ramp are font sizes too: `'9'`, `'12.5'`,
`'28'`, `'42'` and `'96'`, and its pressed fade `'85'` is an opacity; its 7pt
count badge and 15pt media card are the corners `'7'` and `'15'` (`15px` is
the radius apps write most outside the scale). Durations are in milliseconds: Bloom's `instant` 100, `fast` 150,
`normal` 200 and `slow` 300, then the exact durations apps write, `'800'` and
`'2500'` (Willo's photo fades), and `'0'`, which stills a count-up or opens at
once; then the waits Bloom's own components default to, so a screen can name
them: `'400'` (a hover card opens), `'1000'` (a desktop tooltip opens) and
`'3000'` (a connection status says it is reconnecting); and `'4000'`, the
countdown and restored toast the parity cases write. A component's waits take
these tokens, never milliseconds: a hover card's and a tooltip's
`.openDelay()`, a hover card's `.closeDelay()`, a notification's
`.autoDismiss()` and `.introDelay()`, a connection status's
`.reconnectingDelay()` and `.restoredDuration()`. Anything else stays an app's
`defineTokens`. A raw hex colour is an app's
brand colour: the app's own token, never a Bloom one (see [className](#classname)).

The vocabulary is closed. Every value is a name from the spec, whose every rung
`spec.test.ts` holds equal to Bloom's own table, so a rung cannot drift from Bloom.

| Modifier | Values |
| --- | --- |
| `padding(edge?, v)` | space: `none` `2xs` `xs` `sm` `md` `lg` `xl` `2xl`…`5xl`, or a numeric token (`'1'` `'6'` `'10'` `'14'` `'28'` `'44'` `'56'` `'96'`…); `-lg`, `-6` and the like pull outward (a margin); or points computed at render (`depth * 12`) |
| `safeAreaPadding(edge?, source?)` | the device's safe-area insets (`react-native-safe-area-context`), or with `'content'` the content's (what the frame around the view left of them, Bloom's `useContentSafeAreaInsets()`), as padding on those sides: `all` (the default), `top`, `bottom`, `horizontal`, `vertical`, `left`, `right` (physical: the insets are the device's); on the view itself, a top or bottom inset added to the view's own padding there (see [Layout](#layout)) |
| `spacing(v)` | space: the gap between a stack's children |
| `horizontalSpacing(v)`, `verticalSpacing(v)` | space: the gap along one axis only, over `spacing`'s: between children side by side (`columnGap`, `gap-x-*`) or one above another and between wrapped lines (`rowGap`, `gap-y-*`) |
| `childSpacing(axis, v)` | Tailwind's `space-x-*`/`space-y-*` as NativeWind draws them: on the web Tailwind's own rule, a margin after each child but the last along the axis (inline end, or block end); on Android and iOS nothing, as NativeWind writes no rule for a child selector. Not a gap; new screens use `spacing()` |
| `justify(j)`, `wrap()`, `axis(a)` | a stack's distribution: `start` `center` `end` `between` `around` `evenly`; lines that wrap; `horizontal` or `vertical` over its constructor's (see [Layout](#layout)) |
| `frame({ width, height, min…, max… })` | space, or a length of the parent: `full`, `1/2` `1/3` `2/3` `1/4` `3/4` `1/5` `2/5` `3/5`, and the percentages apps write that no fraction holds, named by their value (`90%`, `31%`), or points computed at render (`{ width: itemSize }`; see [Styles computed at runtime](#styles-computed-at-runtime)) |
| `frame({ width: 'fit' })` | as wide (or tall) as its content: `fit-content` (`w-fit`) |
| `frame({ width: 'auto' })` | the view's own size, React Native's and the web's `auto` (`w-auto`, `h-auto`): what a view draws with no length, and what takes back a Bloom component's own length for its content's (a `Field`, full width by default, as wide as its content beside `.grow()`). A width's or a height's, not a minimum's or a maximum's |
| `aspectRatio(r)` | the view's width to its height (`1`, `1.6`, or a ratio computed at render: `media.aspect`), React Native's `aspectRatio`, in its frame's view: the frame's width or height (or the view's stretch) sets the other side. Any view, a pressable one included; an `Image`'s sizes the image in its box |
| `grow()`, `shrink(p)`, `basis(l)` | `never` `normal` `first` (0, 1, 10), or a rank named by its value (`'20'`); a frame length, its `flex-basis` |
| `flex(f)` | Tailwind's flex utility, exactly as NativeWind writes it: `'1'` (`flex-1`: grow 1, shrink 1, basis 0%), `auto`, `initial`, `none`, `grow` (`flex-grow: 1` alone). `grow()` stays React Native's `flex: 1` |
| `margin(edge?, v)` | space (negatives included), or `auto` (the free space on that side: `.margin('leading', 'auto')` is `ms-auto`), on the view itself: no view around it |
| `clipped(on?)` | clips what the view draws to its bounds and its corner; `clipped(false)` draws what overflows it, React Native's `overflow: 'visible'` written out (NativeWind's `overflow-visible`), over a clip the view would otherwise have |
| `align(a)`, `alignSelf(a)` | `start` `center` `end` `stretch` `baseline` (a stack's children; the view's own place) |
| `position({ top, bottom, leading, trailing, left, right, horizontal, vertical })`, `offset({ x, y })` | space, negatives included (see [Layout](#layout)); a position's edge also a percentage of the parent (`'25%'`, `'100%'`: pinned above its parent with `bottom`, the spec's `insetPercent`) and points computed at render (`{ top: insets.top + 8 }`), an offset's axis points computed at render too (`{ y: nudge }`, React Native's `translateY`). `horizontal` is both inline edges at once (React Native's `insetInline`), `vertical` both block edges (`insetBlock`); `.position({})` is a view taken out of the flow where it would sit, pinned by no edge (`position: 'absolute'` alone) |
| `sticky(edge?)` | the web's `position: sticky` at `top` (the default) or `bottom`: held at its scroll container's edge once scrolled to it. React Native has no sticky position, so native draws the view in place; a list's or a scroll's sticky rows are `.stickyIndices()` |
| `cursor(c)` | the pointer's shape over the view on the web (CSS's `cursor`): `auto` `default` `pointer` `grab` `grabbing` `text` `move` `not-allowed`; native has none, as NativeWind's `cursor-*` writes nothing there |
| `zIndex(z)`, `hidden(on?)` | Bloom's `Z_INDEX`: `base` `raised` `floating` `dropdown`, or Tailwind's `'20'` `'30'` `'50'`, or an exact level (`'-1'` `'2'`…`'7'` `'60'` `'1000'`), or `auto` (Tailwind's `z-auto` as NativeWind writes it: `z-index: auto` on the web, nothing on native), or an order computed at render (`count - index`); out of the layout |
| `scale(s)` | drawn scaled about its anchor (its centre unless `anchor` says otherwise): Bloom's `dip` 0.7, `pressed` 0.98, `full`, `swell` 1.2, or a percent (`'95'` `'105'`…) |
| `anchor(a)` | the point the view scales and rotates about (SwiftUI's `anchor:`, Tailwind's `origin-*`): `center` `top` `bottom` `leading` `trailing` `top-leading` `top-trailing` `bottom-leading` `bottom-trailing`; leading and trailing are left and right, as an offset's x |
| `allowsHitTesting(on)`, `accessibilityOnly()` | `false`: touches pass through it and everything in it; `'children'`: only what is inside it takes them, `'self'`: only the view; read by a screen reader and not drawn (`sr-only`) |
| `overlay(view, alignment?)`, `background(view, alignment?)` | a view over or under this one, at `center` `top` `bottom` `leading` `trailing` `top-leading` … `bottom-trailing` |
| `shadow(s)`, `ring(c, width?, offset?)` | Bloom's elevation `s` `m` `floating`, or one of Tailwind's shadows by its exact box-shadow; a colour role (or a role at an opacity, or `none`) as an outline, `thin` `thick` or `'3'` wide |
| `when(condition, (v) => v…)` | a variant: `sm` `md` `lg` `xl` `2xl`, `dark`, `hovered` `focused` `pressed` `disabled`, `web` `native` `ios` `android`, or the app's own boolean (see [Variants](#variants)) |
| `font(f)` | a Bloom type-ramp step (`body`, `headline`, `caption-1`…: size, line height and tracking, as `Text variant`) or a size rung (`sm` 13, `md` 15…), a numeric size (`'9'` `'11'` `'12.5'` `'14'` `'16'` `'18'` `'24'` `'28'` `'30'` `'36'` `'42'` `'96'`), or a face beside the size: `mono` (Bloom's monospaced face), `system-mono` (the platform's own monospaced face, React Native's generic `monospace`) or `display` (the face of Bloom's big ramp steps, for a numeric size: `.font('96').font('display')`) |
| `weight(w)` | `light` `regular` `medium` `semibold` `bold` `extrabold` |
| `tracking(t)` | `tight` `wide` `wider` `widest` (em of the size); or an exact em (`'-0.02em'` `'-0.01em'` `'0.04em'`) or px (`'-0.5px'` … `'2px'`) |
| `leading(l)` | Bloom line heights (ratios of the size) `tight` 1.15, `close` 1.25, `snug` 1.3, `easy` 1.375, `normal` 1.5, `relaxed` 1.625; or an exact px height (`'14px'` … `'40px'`, and `'96px'`, Tailwind's `text-8xl` line, a 96pt countdown's) |
| `foreground(c)`, `background(c)` | colour roles: `foreground` `muted` `primary` `surface` `card` `mutedFill` `border` `error` `destructive` `primaryText` `white` `black`…, or a role at an opacity token (`'muted/70'`); `foreground(undefined)` is no ink of its own (SwiftUI's `foregroundColor(nil)`), so `.foreground(invalid ? 'error' : undefined)` inks the view only when it is invalid |
| `borderStyle(s)` | the border's line: `solid` (the default), `dashed`, `dotted` (React Native's `borderStyle`), in the border's view |
| `border(c, width?, edge?)` | colour role (or a role at an opacity); `hairline` (the platform hairline), `thin` or `thick`, or an exact width (`'0.5'`, a fixed half point; `'1.5'` `'2.5'` `'3'` `'4'`). A colour of `undefined` writes the width alone, as a style with a `borderWidth` and no `borderColor`: the line keeps the platform's default colour (black natively, react-native-web's own on the web), so `.border(subscribed ? 'border' : undefined, 'thin')` is Mention's labelers pill; it reads no theme |
| edges | `padding`, `margin` and `border` take `leading` and `trailing` (the reading direction's sides) and `left` and `right` (physical, the same right to left) |
| `corner(r, side?)`, `cornerCurve(c)` | radius rungs, numeric radii (`'6'` `'7'` `'10'` `'14'` `'15'` `'18'` `'22'`), `full` (Bloom's `radius-max`, 9999; an app's 999 is `full` too, since both round any box under 1998pt fully), `infinite` (Tailwind's `rounded-full` as NativeWind writes it: 9007199254740000 on native, `calc(infinity * 1px)` on the web; a Bloom component that takes a radius in points gets `full`), `concentric`; the corners it rounds: `all`, `top` `bottom` `leading` `trailing`, or one (`top-leading`…), which follow the reading direction, or `left` `right` and one (`top-left`…), which do not (Tailwind's `rounded-l`, `rounded-tl`…); `round` `smooth` (see [Material](#material)) |
| `opacity(o)` | `transparent` 0, `half` 0.5, `opaque` 1; or a percent (`'5'` `'10'` `'20'` `'25'` `'30'` `'40'` `'45'` `'60'` `'70'` `'75'` `'80'` `'85'` `'90'`) |
| `size(s)` | icon rungs, avatar rungs, or Bloom sizes, by node |
| `style(a)` / `shape(s)` / `tint(t)` | Bloom appearance / `circle` `pill` / Bloom tone |
| `glass(material?)` | `regular`, `clear`, `.tint(role, 'subtle' \| 'medium' \| 'strong'?)` |
| `blur()` | Bloom's floating island (`BlurIsland`) |
| `onPress`, `onLongPress(action, duration?)`, `onHover`, `hoverGroup` | handlers. `onPress(undefined)` keeps the pressable host, out of the focus order. `hoverGroup()` on a view with no press of its own is a plain view with Bloom's marker (`hoverGroupProps`), its press a view inside it. A long press's `duration` is how long it is held (React Native's `delayLongPress`: `'500'`, its own default, `'250'`…, the [duration](bloom-language-tokens.mdx#duration) tokens); a Bloom component that takes a long press of its own (a tab, a nav item, a tree item, a destination) takes no duration |
| `accessibilityElement(on)`, `focusable(on?)`, `collapsable(on)` | React Native's own: whether the view is one accessibility element (`accessible`; `false` leaves what is inside to be read, its press kept), whether keyboard focus stops at it (`tabIndex` `0` or `-1`, on the web and Android), and `false` keeps its native view from being flattened (`collapsable`, Android). Each is written on the view's outermost host as the JSX writes it |
| responder props (`onStartShouldSetResponder`, a `PanResponder`'s `panHandlers`) | no modifier: a view that claims or tracks touches by hand stays an `Embed` of its JSX. The language's gestures are `.onPress()`, `.drag()`, `.swipe()`, `.pinch()` and `.onDoubleTap()` |
| `accessibilityState(s)`, `accessibilityValue(v)`, `liveRegion(r)`, `accessibilityHidden(on?)` | what assistive technology reads: a state (`selected`, `checked` or `'mixed'`, `busy`, `expanded`, `disabled`: React Native's `accessibilityState`); a value, a range (`min`, `max`, `now`) or a text (React Native's `accessibilityValue`; numbers and a text, no tokens); a region whose changes are announced (`polite`, `assertive`, `none`: `accessibilityLiveRegion`); hidden from screen readers with what is inside (see [Layout](#layout)) |
| `hitSlop(edge?, s)` | how far beyond its bounds a press reaches: Bloom's `sm` `md` `lg`, `none`, or an exact slop (`'4'` `'5'`); with a side first (`top` `bottom` `horizontal` `vertical` `left` `right`, physical as React Native's insets are) only that side, a later one of a side over the earlier. Every side alike is one number (`hitSlop={8}`), sides of their own React Native's insets with the sides named. An `Action`'s is its `EngagementAction`'s (an `Action` takes no `hitSlop` option) |
| `ref(r)`, `onLayout(f)` | escape hatches, on the view's outermost box (its host, or the Bloom component that draws it): React Native's `ref` (a ref object or a callback, for a visibility tracker, a measurement, a DOM lookup on the web) and `onLayout`. Laying a view out from state its own `onLayout` wrote is discouraged: it draws twice, the second time moved, and can loop; a frame, a grow, an aspect ratio or a `ZStack` says the layout instead |
| `label`, `hint`, `role`, `testID`, `id` | accessibility and identity. `role` is React Native's `accessibilityRole` of the same name: `button` `link` `header` `image` `text` `none` `checkbox` `radio` `tab` `radiogroup` `tablist` `list` `alert` `progressbar` `imagebutton` `summary`; `option` and `listbox`, which React Native names only as a web role, are its `role` (`listbox` is react-native-web's, outside React Native's types; native has neither) (a `Row`'s `.role('option')` is Bloom Item's `role="option"`, whose `.selected()` is `aria-selected`; an option's or a tab's `.accessibilityState({ selected })` is also a flat `aria-selected` on the web, which react-native-web reads in place of the state) |
| `lines(n)`, `noWrap()`, `truncation`, `selectable`, `textStyle`, `link`, `textAlign`, `italic`, `underline`, `strikethrough`, `textCase`, `monospacedDigit`, `wordBreak`, `textShadow` | text only (see [Text](#text)) |
| `listMarker(m)` | a stack only: a web list behind `disc` or `decimal` markers (see [Layout](#layout)) |
| `contentFit`, `aspectRatio`, `tint`, `crossFade`, `onLoad`, `onError` | `Image` only (see [Image](#image)) |
| `contentTransition('roll', direction?)` | text only (see [Animations](#animations)) |
| `count`, `active`, `tint` | `Action` only |
| `trailing(...)`, `countFormat(fn)`, `axis(a)` (a video's vertical column), `tint('default' \| 'onImage')`, `showZeroCounts()` | `ActionBar` only |

Each node type exposes only the modifiers that apply to it, so the editor
completes `Text(...).` with text modifiers and rejects `HStack().lines(2)`,
`.padding(12)` and `.foreground('#f00')` at compile time.

**Densities.** `.density()` is a node's own: each node in the spec lists its
Bloom component's names for its densities, and the types and the compiler
check a node's density against its own list. A button's are Bloom's control
densities (`default`, `compact`), a row's, a list's and the product rows'
Bloom's content ones (`comfortable`, `compact`), a listing grid's `results`
and `compact`; a component whose compactness is a flag (a chat header, a
calendar) takes `default` and `compact`. So a name means one Bloom value
wherever it is accepted: `Row('Ana').density('default')` and
`Button(...).density('comfortable')` are compile errors.

**App tokens.** When a screen being ported uses a measurement that Bloom's scales
lack, the app names it once with `defineTokens`. It is still a name at each call
site:

```ts
const row = defineTokens({ space: { context: 18 }, iconSize: { context: 13 } });
Icon(RiRepeat2Line).size(row.iconSize.context);
```

Mention's row needed seven of them. Two are Bloom rungs now: the 64px body
gutter is `'64'` and `leading-tight` (1.25) is `close`. The 18px context row,
the 3px gap before its text and the 13, 14 and 18px glyphs stay app tokens.

An app's brand colour is an app token too: `defineTokens({ color: { blue:
'#1877f2' } })` declares a colour, the same in both schemes, and
`.background()`, `.foreground()` (text and glyphs), `.border()` and `.ring()`
take it where they take a role. It is a hex, an `rgb()` or `rgba()` of numbers
(`'rgba(255,255,255,0.72)'`), or a CSS colour name React Native paints too
(`'white'`; the spec's `appColorName`, every CSS name but none React Native
alone knows), checked when it is declared. It paints as that string on every
platform (inline on the web: no class names it), so it draws what the same
colour written in JSX draws. It varies as a role does: under `when()` it is
the colour while the condition holds, on the web through the view's JS-picked
style (see [Variants](#variants), a value with no class). The paints that are
props of a Bloom component (`.tint()`, `.fill()`, `.stroke()`, a button's
`.colors()`, a glass tint, at rest and in a variant) take roles only: a
material or a control computes its look from a role.

## Layout

```ts
HStack(Avatar(user.avatar), Text(user.name)).justify('between').wrap().spacing('sm')
VStack(...).frame({ width: 'full', maxWidth: '1/2' }).alignSelf('center')
Avatar(user.avatar).overlay(Badge(3).tint('danger'), 'top-trailing')
VStack(...).background(Image(cover).frame({ width: 'full', height: 'full' }))
Button(Icon(RiCloseLine)).position({ top: 'sm', trailing: 'sm' }).zIndex('floating')
VStack(...).background('surface').corner('xl').shadow('m')
```

- **Distribution.** `justify` is the main axis (Compose's arrangement):
  packed at `start`, `center` or `end`, or the free space `between`, `around` or
  `evenly` around the children. `spacing` is only the gap. `wrap()` flows
  children that do not fit onto further lines. `axis()` lays a stack out along
  the other axis, which is what a breakpoint variant uses (`flex-col
  md:flex-row`).
- **Gaps per axis.** `.horizontalSpacing(v)` is the gap between children side
  by side (`columnGap`), `.verticalSpacing(v)` the gap between children one
  above another and between a wrapped row's lines (`rowGap`). Each is over
  `spacing`'s on its axis, as React Native's `columnGap` and `rowGap` are over
  `gap`.
- **Self alignment.** `alignSelf` is the view's own place across its parent's
  axis, over the parent's `align`. `mx-auto` in a column is `alignSelf('center')`.
- **Safe area.** `.safeAreaPadding(edge?)` is SwiftUI's: the view padded by
  the device's safe area on those sides, as an app pads by
  `useSafeAreaInsets()` (`paddingTop: insets.top`). It reads the insets as it
  renders (none outside a `SafeAreaProvider`) and pads the view it is chained
  on, as a padding does, so the view's fill, flex and own padding stay one
  view: a top or bottom inset adds to the view's padding there, and
  `paddingTop: insets.top + 24` is `.safeAreaPadding('top').padding('top', '2xl')`.
  As with a padding, a fill chained after it covers the inset
  (`.safeAreaPadding('vertical').background('card')`) and one chained before it
  does not. A left or right inset beside a padding across is a view of its
  own around it (a padding's start and end are logical, the inset physical).
  On a column or row with no press, the safe area is the host itself. The insets are the device's
  physical ones, not what a Bloom header or bar has already taken; `.safeAreaPadding(edge,
  'content')` pads by what is left (`useContentSafeAreaInsets()`, as a screen's body pads
  itself): `paddingTop: insets.top + 60` from it is `.safeAreaPadding('top',
  'content').padding('top', '5xl')`.
- **Auto margins.** `.margin(edge, 'auto')` takes the free space on that side,
  as React Native's and CSS's `margin: auto` do: `.margin('leading', 'auto')`
  pushes the view to its row's trailing end (Tailwind's `ms-auto`), and
  `.margin('auto')` centres it on both axes. A padding has no `auto`.
- **Lengths.** A frame takes a length of its parent (`full` is `100%`, `1/2` is
  `50%`) besides space rungs. `w-full` is `.frame({ width: 'full' })`: the same
  value, not SwiftUI's `maxWidth: .infinity`, so no rendered size moves.
- **Depth.** `ZStack(...)` is SwiftUI's: its children over one another, later
  ones on top, the stack as big as its largest child, and each child at
  `.alignment(a)` (`ZStack(alignment:)`: `center` by default, `top-leading`,
  `bottom` and the rest of the overlay alignments). A child pinned to its
  parent (`Fill()`, `MediaInsetBorder()`, `.position()`) covers the whole stack
  and takes no part in its size, as a flexible view fills SwiftUI's. React
  Native's layout has no box sized by the largest of overlapping children, so
  each child is in a slot of the stack's width (the stack is as wide as the
  widest child, and the slot places it across) and the slots share one line:
  each slot's margins take back its own height, so none moves another, and the
  stack's box is as tall as the tallest slot. Each slot's height is the one its
  own layout reports (`onLayout`, on every platform), kept in state; a margin
  never changes a slot's height, so the layout it causes settles, and a child
  that grows reports again. Until the slots report, the children are in their
  slots unmoved, one under another. A slot is `static`, so
  a pinned child is pinned to the stack. A child that should span the stack
  says so (`.frame({ width: 'full' })`). A `ZStack` takes no `spacing`,
  `align` or `justify`: it has no axis.
- **Overlays.** `.overlay(view, alignment)` draws a view over this one, as
  SwiftUI's `overlay(alignment:)` does: a box the size of the view as drawn so
  far (its padding and frame before the overlay included), the view placed at
  the alignment. `.background(view, alignment)` is the same box under the
  content. They sit in the layer order between the frame and the fill, so
  `.padding('md').overlay(x)` covers the padding and `.overlay(x).padding('md')`
  does not. The box lets touches through outside its own content.
- **Pinned views.** `.position(edges)` takes the view out of its stack's flow
  and pins it at those distances from its parent's edges (React Native's
  absolute position); an overlay is the way to put one view on another, and
  `position` is for what is truly absolute. `.offset({ x, y })` draws the view
  shifted (`x` right, `y` down, physical) without moving its siblings.
- **Stacking.** `zIndex` takes Bloom's own levels (`Z_INDEX`): `base` 0,
  `raised` 1, `floating` 10, `dropdown` 40; Tailwind's `z-20`, `z-30` and
  `z-50` are the numeric tokens `'20'`, `'30'`, `'50'`, and the exact levels
  apps write are named by their value: `'-1'` (under its siblings), `'2'` to
  `'7'`, `'60'`, and `'1000'`, a picker raised over a whole screen (Mention's
  mention picker, `z-[1000]`).
- **Defaults that are no modifier.** Every view is the positioned parent of
  what it pins (`position: relative`, React Native's and react-native-web's
  default) and draws what overflows it (`overflow: visible`, the default of
  both): neither is written, and the className table drops `relative` and
  `overflow-visible`. `.clipped()` is the one that changes it.
- **Elevation.** `.shadow(role)` is Bloom's `bloomShadowStyle(role)`: the web's
  box-shadow, native's shadow and elevation (`floating` is a box-shadow on
  both). It is the outermost layer. A shadow never shares a view with a
  clip, since iOS clips a view's own shadow: after a `.clipped()` corner it is a
  view of its own around the clipped box, with the same radii. Tailwind's own
  shadows are tokens named by their exact box-shadow
  (`.shadow('0 1px 3px 0 rgb(0 0 0 / 0.1), 0 1px 2px -1px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.1)')`
  is `shadow-sm`), drawn as that box-shadow on every platform.
- **Corners per side.** `.corner(r, side)` rounds only those corners
  (`rounded-t-3xl` is `.corner('3xl', 'top')`); `leading` and `trailing`
  follow the reading direction, as the edges do. Corners of other sides join
  one view; a corner rounded twice wraps, as a padding of the same edge does.
  A glass or an island takes one radius, so a corner of some sides only is
  left out on it.
- **Scale and basis.** `.scale(s)` draws the view scaled about its centre,
  after its offset (Tailwind's translate, then rotate, then scale), without
  moving its siblings; `.anchor(point)` moves the point it scales and rotates
  about (`origin-top-left` is `.anchor('top-leading')`). `.basis(length)` is
  its `flex-basis` (`basis-0` is `.basis('none')`).
- **Hit testing.** `.allowsHitTesting(false)` lets touches and the pointer
  through the view and everything inside it, as SwiftUI's does
  (`pointer-events-none`); `true` takes them again. `'children'` lets them
  through the view's outermost box but not what is inside it (React Native's
  `box-none`, a scrim whose buttons still press), and `'self'` takes them on
  that box and not inside it (`box-only`). `undefined` sets none, as React
  Native's `pointerEvents={undefined}`, so `.allowsHitTesting(split ?
  'children' : undefined)` lets touches through only while it holds. On the
  web each is the class react-native-web's own `pointerEvents` prop draws
  (`box-none`: `pointer-events: none` on the view, `auto` on each child), held
  equal to it by the layout parity, a value chosen at render included;
  react-native-web drops an inline `pointerEvents`, so on the web a Bloom
  component's is a box of its own around it. React Native's `pointerEvents`
  written in an inline style is a native hit testing: react-native-web writes
  `box-none` and `box-only` there as they are, CSS values the browser drops,
  so the web view takes touches as any view does. Its form is
  `.when('native', (v) => v.allowsHitTesting('children'))`, held equal to the
  inline style by the layout parity; a StyleSheet's style is compiled into
  the prop's classes, and is `.allowsHitTesting()` on every platform.
- **Drawn, not read.** `.accessibilityHidden()` hides the view and everything
  inside it from screen readers, each platform its own way: on Android and iOS
  `accessibilityElementsHidden` and `importantForAccessibility:
  'no-hide-descendants'`, on the web `aria-hidden` (react-native-web drops the
  native props). `.accessibilityHidden(false)` reads it again (on the web it
  writes nothing). `.accessibilityState({ … })` and `.liveRegion(r)` pass
  React Native's `accessibilityState` and `accessibilityLiveRegion` as an app
  writes them, on every platform: react-native-web reads the live region
  (`aria-live`) and, as for the JSX they replace, no state.
  `.accessibilityValue({ min, max, now })` or `({ text })` is React Native's
  `accessibilityValue`, on the same box: a progress or a slider's place in its
  range, or a value in words.
- **Read, not drawn.** `.accessibilityOnly()` is Tailwind's `sr-only`: a
  1pt clipped box out of the layout that a screen reader still announces.
  Native has no clip path or white-space, so it is the box alone, as
  NativeWind drew it there.
- **Web lists.** `.listMarker('disc' | 'decimal')` on a stack makes it an
  HTML list and each child a list item, so the browser draws its markers
  (`list-disc`); give it the room with a leading padding. Native draws no
  markers, as React Native has none.
- **Dashed and dotted borders.** `.borderStyle('dashed' | 'dotted')` draws the
  border of its view with that line (React Native's `borderStyle`; `solid` is
  the default). It joins the border's view, so its place in the chain is the
  border's.
- **Rings.** `.ring(role, width?, offset?)` is an outline (Bloom's focus ring
  recipe): it draws outside the box and takes no room. `ring('none')` is the
  web's `outline: none`. It sits in the border's layer.
- **Hidden.** `.hidden()` takes the view out of the layout (`display: none`)
  and keeps it mounted, unlike SwiftUI's `hidden()`, which keeps its room. A
  child that is not there is a conditional child (`show && view`); `hidden`
  is for a variant (`.hidden().when('md', (v) => v.hidden(false))`).

Not verified on a device: a ring (React Native's `outline*`, 0.77+) and a
shadow beside a clip on iOS and Android.

## Variants

```ts
VStack(...).padding('sm').when('md', (v) => v.padding('lg'))
HStack(...).axis('vertical').when('md', (v) => v.axis('horizontal').justify('between'))
VStack(...).hidden().when('lg', (v) => v.hidden(false))
Card().background('surface').when('dark', (v) => v.background('popover'))
HStack(...).onPress(open).when('pressed', (v) => v.opacity('70')).when('hovered', (v) => v.foreground('foreground'))
VStack(...).when('web', (v) => v.padding('lg'))   // a platform: settled when it renders
HStack(...).background('mutedFill').when(isActive, (v) => v.background('primary'))   // the app's own boolean
```

`.when(condition, build)` is SwiftUI's environment-driven variant as a
modifier: `build` chains style modifiers onto the view, and while the
condition holds they **override the values the chain set before the call**
(Tailwind's `p-2 md:p-4`, not a second padding). Each goes where it
overrides: a box modifier into the view that set the same thing last (a
padding of the same edge, a border of the same side), else the outermost
view so far; a text trait into the environment, so it reaches the text
inside; a stack's layout to its host; a placement to its outermost box.

| Condition | Holds | Web | Native |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `sm` `md` `lg` `xl` `2xl` | the window at that tier of Bloom's `BREAKPOINTS` or wider | `@media (min-width: …)` | the leaf reads `useWindowBreakpoint()`, which re-renders it only across a breakpoint |
| `dark` | the dark scheme | `.dark &` (Bloom puts `dark` on the root) | the leaf reads the scheme of the palette it paints from |
| `hovered` `focused` `pressed` | the view's own state (`focused` is a keyboard's focus, `:focus-visible`) | the view's outermost box carries the anchor class `blsg`, and a class answers its nearest anchor's state (see below) | the outermost box (`StateHost`) tracks the states its variants answer and hands them to the view's leaves |
| `disabled` | the view's own `.disabled()` (Button, FAB, a pressable stack) | settled when it renders | settled when it renders |
| `web` `native` `ios` `android` | the platform | settled | settled |
| a boolean (`isActive`) | the app's own condition, read as the view renders | settled | settled |

- **No view body runs.** A live condition is answered below the view: by CSS
  on the web, by the leaf that draws each host on native (`Conditioned`,
  which reads the breakpoint and the state and paints from its tree's
  palette), which builds its props once per palette and set of holding variants.
- **The variant sheets** hold only the variant classes that have rendered.
  A variant is a generated class again under its condition (`<class>-md`,
  `<class>-hovered`…); the first time a style naming one is resolved (styles
  are cached by key, so once), its rule joins its condition's constructed
  sheet, kept in the generated sheet's order. One sheet per live condition,
  placed after the language's own in the order they override, so a page
  parses one rule per variant class it draws: the Variants parity story
  draws 13 (about 1.4 KB), where a whole sheet per condition was 1,806 rules
  and 69 to 186 KB each. A breakpoint's rule has the base's specificity and
  comes after it (a wider breakpoint after a narrower); the scheme's selector
  is one class stronger and a state's two, so a state beats the scheme and a
  press beats a hover.
- **A value with no class** (an app token: a brand colour, an app's space or
  length) cannot vary in CSS. A view whose variants hold one is varied on the
  web the way a Bloom component is (below): its style is an object a
  `Conditioned` leaf picks from the breakpoint, the scheme and its state, its
  outermost box tracks its press, hover and focus in JavaScript, and that box
  still carries the anchor and press host classes, so the CSS of the views
  inside it answers it as before. Native is unchanged: every leaf picks.
- **No build step.** The compiler checks every variant (its condition, its
  modifiers, each value) but emits no CSS: which classes a variant draws
  depends on where it lands (the text traits a font, weight or leading
  variant combines with, the layer a box modifier joins, the corner a
  shadow follows), which only the runtime resolves. Emitting at build time
  would be a second resolver; the runtime's rule per class is the same
  bytes, and only for what renders.
- **What varies** is the spec's `variants.modifiers`: box, text and stack
  style, placement (a basis and hit testing included), elevation, rings and
  geometry (`.scale()`, `.offset()`, `.rotation()`). Under a
  condition that holds as the view renders (`web`, `native`, `ios`, `android`,
  `disabled`, a boolean that is `true`), anything else but a box layer, a placement, a press or a name
  is the chain's own, as if chained there (`when('web', (v) => v.monospacedDigit())`
  is Tailwind's `tabular-nums` as NativeWind draws it; `when('native', (v) =>
  v.accessibilityHidden())` hides a view from native screen readers only, as React
  Native's `accessibilityElementsHidden` and `importantForAccessibility` do). What no
  condition sets is the spec's `variants.held.except`, which the compiler reads too.
  Anything else inside `when()` is left out and said once in development; the
  compiler rejects it (`A variant cannot change .onPress()`), and checks the
  condition and every value.
- **Geometry varies by component.** A transform is one property, so a view
  whose geometry varies draws it as its outermost box's placement, one value
  per component (translate x, translate y, rotate, scale), and a variant
  overrides only the components it sets (`active:scale-95 hover:-translate-y-1`
  holds both while both do). Native composes the components into one
  `transform` after the variants that hold; on the web each component is a
  custom property class (`--bl-tx`, `--bl-ty`, `--bl-r`, `--bl-s`) composed by
  one `transform` class of the components the view has, so a hover, press or
  breakpoint class sets only its property. A component a variant sets rests at
  its identity. Resting geometry draws the same way (the same transform it
  always computed). Under `.animation()` the change plays: on native the box
  whose state the variant answers is itself the animated host, on the web the
  transform's CSS transition. Geometry a `MotionValue`, a gesture or press
  feedback moves takes no variant (the one mapper writes it): its variants are
  left out, said once, and a `MotionValue` inside `when()` is a compiler error.
- **A Bloom component** (Avatar, Button, Image…) reads its style as an
  object, which CSS cannot vary, so on every platform its variants go into
  its own style, picked by a `Conditioned` leaf from the window's
  breakpoint, the scheme and its state (on the web each role is its CSS
  variable, as in its inline style). No view is put around it, so its layout
  is the one it has without a variant. Its state is tracked by its box in
  JavaScript on the web too (the component takes `onPressIn`/`onHoverIn`…
  as Bloom's Button, its FAB, Avatar and EngagementAction do; a Badge, an IconCircle, a
  Loading, a Divider, an AvatarGroup or a button group takes no press of its
  own, so its press, hover and focus are a language host's around it, whose
  box tracks them the same way), and its `focused` is the browser's
  `:focus-visible`, read when the focus arrives, as a view's selector is: a
  click's focus is not a keyboard's.
- **States are the view's**, as SwiftUI's: the anchor is the view's
  outermost box, and a class answers its NEAREST anchor (its own box or the
  closest around it), as a native leaf reads the nearest press host. A
  state is the innermost pressable's, as react-native-web's Pressable
  takes it (a hover or a press of an inner pressable is not the outer
  one's): the selector is the anchor in that state with no pressable in
  the same state inside it (`:has()`), and no anchor between it and the
  class (the spec's `variants.selectors`; the `:where()` parts add no
  specificity). A pressable is what react-native-web draws as one: an
  element with Bloom's press host class (`blsp`, `PRESS_HOST_CLASS` in
  `@oxy.so/bloom/styles`: every pressable host of the language, and Bloom's
  Avatar and EngagementAction), or any element react-native-web makes
  focusable (`tabindex="0"`: each Pressable it draws that is not disabled,
  an `Embed`'s or another Bloom component's included). Bloom's web Button
  and FAB are DOM buttons, which react-native-web does not keep a hover
  from, so a hover of one is the stack's too, as it is in Bloom JSX. So a
  pressable inside another, a Bloom one or an embedded one included,
  answers only its own press and hover, on every platform (the parity
  cases, held in Chrome by `verify-parity-web.mjs`, which holds every
  anchor, press host and focusable element). A text trait a variant sets
  above a nested pressable answers that nearest one on both platforms.
- **Icons** follow a varied foreground. On the web the glyph paints
  `currentColor` and its colour is a class under each condition (CSS answers
  a hover, as it does for text); on native the glyph's leaf is a
  `Conditioned` one that fills it from the environment.
- **A material's variants.** A glass, an island or a concentric box is a
  Bloom material drawn from its own props. A varied padding of one is a view
  just inside it, the same room, which takes the variant. Each also varies
  its frame and border (the material's own style; an island takes no border
  width, as `BlurIsland` draws its own edge), its corner (the radius a glass
  or an island clips to: one radius, so a corner of some sides is left out;
  a concentric box's corner is its parent's less the inset, so it has none
  to vary) and its fill (a glass's `.background(token)` is Surface's `fill`,
  the lens's tint, Liquid Glass's `tintColor`, a role over the glass's own
  tint while it holds; an island's and a concentric box's is their
  `backgroundColor`). They reach the material as its style and props,
  picked by a `Conditioned` leaf from the breakpoint, the scheme and the
  state, built once per palette and set of holding variants (nothing is
  resolved per render); a state the material answers is tracked by the box
  around it, which is also the anchor of the CSS of the views inside it.
  Under a Button or FAB the control takes the press, so the control reports
  the states its glass answers (`Reporting`, beside its own handlers) to a
  box around the glass (`ReportedState`), whose state the glass's leaf
  reads. A material is never faded (an opacity under `when()` is left out:
  an opacity on a material host or its ancestor takes its backdrop blur
  away), said once in development.
- **Variants nest.** `when('md', (v) => v.when('hovered', (h) => h.padding('lg')))`
  holds while both do (`md:hover:`): native reads both; the web writes the
  widest breakpoint's query around the other terms' selectors, each state's
  selector around the class (both answer its nearest anchor, so two states
  are one view's) and the scheme's around the states'. A joined condition's
  sheet comes after the single ones, and its selector is at least as specific
  as its strongest term's.

## Text

`Text(...runs)` is Bloom's `Text`: the type ramp, Bloom's families (Inter, and
the display face for `large-title`, `display-*` and `title-*`), the desktop
scale under a `TypographyScope`, and Bloom's ink when nothing sets a colour.

```ts
Text('Notifications').font('title-1').weight('bold')
Text(post.text).font('body').lines(3)
Text('Settings').textStyle('h2')
Text('Read the ', Text('terms').link('https://oxy.so/terms'), '.').font('caption-1')
```

- **Type.** `.font(family)` with `.weight(w)` is Bloom's `variant`
  (`body` + `semibold` is `body-semibold`; `regular` when no weight is set). A
  size rung (`.font('sm')`) sets the size alone, and a weight or a leading
  only when given. `.leading()` on a ramp font replaces its line height.
- **Bloom's typography components.** `.textStyle(name)` draws one: `title`,
  `subtitle`, `h1`…`h6`, `paragraph` (`P`), `lead`, `large`, `small`, `muted`,
  `blockquote`. Each keeps what the component brings: a heading's role and
  `aria-level`, `H2`'s rule, a paragraph's breakpoint spacing, `Lead`'s and
  `Muted`'s ink. A font of the chain is the component's `variant`, as Bloom's
  own props merge. A quotation is a box around its text: its layers go on the
  box, and what the chain sets for the text is a span inside it.
- **Truncation.** `.lines(n)` is `numberOfLines`, cut at the tail;
  `.truncation('head' | 'middle' | 'tail' | 'clip')` says where.
  `.noWrap()` keeps a web line from wrapping. `.truncation('tail')` with no
  line limit is the web's ellipsis where the text is cut (`text-overflow`);
  React Native draws one only at a line limit. Tailwind's `truncate` is
  `.noWrap().clipped().truncation('tail')`, as NativeWind draws it (only the
  clip on native).
- **Selection.** `.selectable()` lets the reader select and copy it.
- **Links.** `.link(href)` is Bloom's inline link, as its Markdown draws one:
  the primary ink and an underline, announced as a link, an anchor on the web
  (a page of another site opens beside the app) and opened outside the app on
  native. Its own `.foreground()` replaces the ink, its own `.onPress()` the
  opening.
- **Lines and marks.** `.textAlign('start' | 'center' | 'end' | 'justify' |
  'left' | 'right')` (`start` and `end` follow the reading direction; `left`
  and `right` are Tailwind's `text-left` and `text-right` as NativeWind writes
  them, React Native's own `left` and `right` and the web's CSS ones), `.italic()`,
  `.underline()`, `.strikethrough()`, `.textCase('uppercase' | 'lowercase' |
  'capitalize')`. On the web the alignment stays inline: react-native-web
  writes it per direction, which one class cannot.
- **Spans.** A `Text` inside a `Text` is a React Native span, as Bloom's
  Markdown nests its runs: it writes only what its own chain names (a weight,
  an ink, a link) and inherits the rest, so a span of an `h1` keeps the
  heading's size.
- **Pressable runs.** A span takes `.onPress()` and `.onLongPress()`, as a
  React Native `Text` nested in a text takes `onPress`: it stays a span of the
  line (no box of its own), a URL that opens on a press or a "Read more"
  (`RawText(body, Text(' Read more').foreground('primary').onPress(expand))`).
  A whole `Text` takes them too, a footer link.
- **Element runs.** `Embed(element)` is a run like any other, drawn as it is
  inside the line: a router's `Link`, an app's own component. A render
  function hands an element that takes a child (`asChild`) a span: inside a
  text, `render(Text(…))` draws the span's host, so expo-router's `Link
  asChild` gives it its `href`, its role and its press (an anchor on the web),
  and its own props (`hrefAttrs` with `rel: 'nofollow'`) reach the span as
  they reach a JSX child:

  ```tsx
  const mention = (handle: string) =>
    Embed((render) => (
      <Link href={`/@${handle}`} push asChild>
        {render(Text(`@${handle}`).foreground('primary'))}
      </Link>
    ));
  RawText('Hi ', mention('bob'), ', see ', Text('oxy.so').foreground('primary').onPress(open))
  ```
- **Raw text.** `RawText(...runs)` is React Native's own `Text`, not Bloom's:
  it writes only what its chain names, so a text that names no family draws
  the platform face (Roboto on Android, San Francisco on iOS,
  react-native-web's system stack on the web), not Inter. It is what the
  codemod writes for a React Native `Text`, so the move changes no pixel: a
  face the app's bundler gives React Native's `Text` (`@oxy.so/app-preset`'s
  `baseFont`) reaches `RawText` alike. New screens use `Text`. Inside a `Text`
  it is a span that inherits its parent's face. It takes nothing set above it,
  so a root or a `view()` body over one reads no environment, and the theme
  only when a role inks it, as React Native's `Text` in a component that reads
  the theme to ink it.

- **Digits and breaking.** `.monospacedDigit()` draws digits of one width
  (`tabular-nums`, React Native's `fontVariant`). `.wordBreak('all')` lets a
  web line break anywhere (`break-all`), `.wordBreak('words')` only inside a
  word too long for its line (`break-words`); React Native has no such
  breaking, so native text keeps its own, as NativeWind drew it there.
- **Mono.** `.font('mono')` is Bloom's monospaced face, the family
  `font-mono` gives Bloom's `Text` (`fontFamilyStyle('mono')`, exported from
  `@oxy.so/bloom/typography`). A family sits beside the size: it keeps the
  ramp step or size rung the text has, and a later size keeps the face, so
  `.font('body').font('mono')` is body in mono.
- **The platform's mono.** `.font('system-mono')` is the platform's own
  monospaced face by its generic name (`fontFamily: 'monospace'`, as an app
  writes it), not Bloom's JetBrains Mono: a slug or an id drawn as the system
  draws code. `.when('native', (v) => v.font('system-mono'))` keeps the web
  in the face it inherits.
- **Display.** `.font('display')` is the face Bloom's `Text` sets its big
  ramp steps in (`large-title`, `display-*`, `title-*`: BlomusModernus,
  `fontFamilyStyle('display')`), for a size off the ramp: Mention's camera
  countdown is `.font('96').leading('96px').weight('bold').font('display')`.
  It is a family as `mono` is, beside the size; on Android and iOS Bloom's
  text host draws the cut its weight selects (the bold cut at `bold`), as it
  does for a ramp step. A ramp step already has its face: `display` is for a
  numeric size.
- **Tracking.** `.tracking(t)` is letter spacing in em of the text's size,
  Tailwind's `tracking-*`: `tight` −0.025,
  `wide` 0.025, `wider` 0.05, `widest` 0.1, or an exact em or px token (an
  em token measures from the size, a px one does not). It is a
  trait of the environment, as `leading` is. Both measure from the text's
  size: its font's, or with no font the 13px of Bloom's `Text` without a
  variant (the size itself stays Bloom's, unwritten). Over a ramp step it
  replaces the step's own tracking.
- **Shadow.** `.textShadow(shadow)` draws a shadow under the glyphs, for text
  over a photo or a video. Each is named by its exact CSS `text-shadow`
  (`'0 1px 3px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.8)'`, `'0 1px 2px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.8)'`): React
  Native's `textShadowColor`, `textShadowOffset` and `textShadowRadius` as an
  app writes them, which react-native-web draws as that `text-shadow`.

## Icons

`Icon(X)` draws any Bloom icon component (`@oxy.so/bloom/icons`: Remix, the
Simple Icons brand marks and Bloom's own set, 588 today), as `<X size fill />`
draws it.

```ts
Icon(RiHeart3Line).size('sm').foreground('error')
HStack(Icon(RiRepeat2Line), Text('Reposted')).foreground('muted')
```

- **Size** is a rung of Bloom's icon scale (`2xs` 8, `xs` 12, `sm` 16, `md` 20,
  the default, `lg` 24, `xl` 28, `2xl` 32, `3xl` 48), a numeric side carried
  over from apps (`'10'` `'11'` `'13'` `'14'` `'15'` `'17'` `'18'` `'21'` `'22'`
  `'40'` `'44'` `'64'` `'96'`: `.size('18')` is `<X width={18} height={18} />`),
  or an app token (`defineTokens({ iconSize: { context: 13 } })`). An
  `IconCircle` takes the named rungs only, as Bloom's `size` names them.
- **Colour** is the foreground: the icon's own `.foreground()`, else the
  environment's, else `foreground`. A bare Bloom icon element paints
  `primary` when given no fill; a language icon follows the text around it,
  as SwiftUI's does, so `Icon(X)` alone is `<X fill={colors.text} />`.
- **A box** (padding, a fill, a corner, a name) is one `View` around the glyph.
  A glyph that is only placed (a margin, a flex, a pin) takes its placement as
  its own style, as the Svg an app writes takes `style={{ marginLeft: 2 }}`,
  with no view around it.
- **`currentColor`** is the glyph's `color` on every platform: react-native-svg
  and Bloom's native glyph resolve it so (black with none), as CSS does on the
  web. `<X fill="currentColor" color={c} />` is `Icon(X).foreground(c)`.
- Bloom's icon `gradient` prop has no modifier: no app passes one.

## Material

Bloom's material is the glass: the platform's own (the lens, Liquid Glass) or
Bloom's `Surface` and its blur where the platform has none (see
[Glass](#glass)). Glass is for the navigation layer (bars, toolbars, floating
controls), never for content such as this card (see
[Liquid Glass rules](#liquid-glass-rules)). The rest of the material family
are modifiers on any view and three nodes.

```ts
Card(
  Text('Storage').font('headline'),
```
