```ts
  Divider(),
  Grid(...usage.map((item) => UsageCard({ item }))).columns(3).spacing('md'),
  VStack(AspectRatio(16 / 9, Image(cover))).corner('concentric'),
)
  .padding('md')
  .corner('2xl')
  .cornerCurve('smooth')
```

- **Concentric corners.** `.corner('concentric')` is Bloom's concentric
  geometry: the radius of the shape the box sits in, less the inset between
  them (a glass, a Bloom card or panel, another concentric box), drawn by
  `ConcentricView` (on a glass, `Surface radius="concentric"`). It clips
  with `.clipped()`, as any corner does.
- **Smooth corners.** `.cornerCurve('smooth')` is Bloom's platform-adaptive
  corner (`shapes`' `surfaceStyle`): continuous on iOS, a squircle on the web,
  round on Android; on a glass, Surface's `cornerCurve`. It sits in the
  corner's layer.
- **A glass's corner is its radius.** Surface clips itself, so the corner of a
  glass is Surface's `radius`, not a style of its host.
- **`Divider(label?)`** is Bloom's Divider, in its stack's direction:
  vertical in an `HStack`, as SwiftUI's is. `.lineStyle('single' | 'double' |
  'fill')`, `.align('start' | 'center' | 'end')` (the label's place),
  `.spacing(space)` (the room on each side), `.tint(role)` (the line). A label
  `Text` with nothing of its own is Bloom's divider label (its type and ink);
  any other view is drawn as it is. In a `Menu` it is the line between rows. `.axis('vertical')` or `.axis('horizontal')` says which way the line runs over its stack's (Bloom's `vertical`): a vertical line outside an `HStack`.
- **`Fill(...)`** covers the whole of the view it is in (a scrim, an overlay).
- **`AspectRatio(ratio, ...)`** is its parent's width and the height `ratio`
  (width over height) gives; a ratio that is not one yet (`0 / 0` before an
  image loads) is a square, as Bloom's is.
- **`Grid(...cells)`** is Bloom's `ResponsiveGrid`: `.columns(n)` the most
  columns, `.minCellWidth(space)` a cell's narrowest width (an app token for
  the widths apps use, 128 to 300), `.spacing(space)`, `.flow('rows' |
  'columns')`, `.stretchItems()` (its chart cards fill the tallest cell of a
  row of several columns, Bloom's `stretchItems`).

- **Islands.** `.blur()` floats the view over content as Bloom's
  `BlurIsland`: the shared material with its shadow, drawn by the lens on
  Android 13+ where the device has it. It is a capsule unless the layer's
  `.corner()` names a radius, and takes the layer's `cornerCurve`; a
  `.background(role)` in the layer is its fill, painted from the theme on
  every platform (the island composites its tint for what sits on it). It
  sits in the fill's layer, as `.glass()` does: `.padding('sm').blur()` is
  one island, `.blur().padding('sm')` pads outside it. A `Button` or `FAB`
  inside it paints with the island's shared material (Bloom's
  `material="shared"`, as Bloom's own controls in a `BlurIsland` do), across
  a `view()` too: the environment carries the island, and a glass inside it
  ends it.

  ```ts
  HStack(Icon(RiFilterLine), Text('Filters')).spacing('xs').padding('sm').blur()
  ```

- **`Shape(name)`** is one of Bloom's silhouettes (`circle`, `squircle` and
  the named shapes) at a fixed side, `.size(space)` (`4xl`, 40, by default):
  `.fill(role)` (the default, in the foreground) is Bloom's `shapes` `Fill`,
  `.stroke(role, width?)` its `Border` (an outline inside the silhouette, a
  border width), `.image(source, alt?)` its `Image` (clipped to it). The last
  of the three in the chain is what it draws; two are an overlay
  (`Shape('heart').fill('primary').overlay(Shape('heart').stroke('border'))`).
  A silhouette takes no style, so its box is a view around it.

A glass is the platform's own material (see [Glass](#glass)); an island is
Bloom's.

## Button

`Button(...content)` is Bloom's `Button` in every variant apps use:

```ts
Button(Text('Save')).style('solid').tint('accent').size('md').onPress(save)
Button(Icon(RiAddLine), Text('New post')).loading(saving)
Button(Text('Next'), Icon(RiArrowRightLine)).style('outline')
Button(Icon(RiMoreFill)).style('subtle').shape('circle').label('More')
Button(Text('Bold')).style('subtle').pressed(bold).onPress(toggleBold)
Button(Text('Docs')).style('plain').link('https://oxy.so/docs').underline()
Button(Text('Continue with Oxy')).colors('primary', 'onPrimary')
Button(Text('Terms')).style('plain').linkTone('text')
ButtonGroup(Button(Text('Day')), Button(Text('Week'))).separated().size('sm')
```

- **Content.** An `Icon` before the `Text` is the leading glyph, one after it
  the trailing glyph; the `Text` runs are the label. A glyph with an ink of
  its own (`Icon(X).foreground(role)`) is drawn as it is. A child that is
  neither an `Icon` nor a `Text` makes the children the button's content,
  drawn as views in the button (Bloom's `children`), none read as a glyph or
  label: `Button(Loading().style('inline').size('sm')).label(name)`, or a glyph
  as content rather than as the button's icon, `Button(Group(Icon(X)))`.
- **Look.** `.style('solid' | 'subtle' | 'outline' | 'plain')`, `.tint(tone)`
  (Bloom's tones, `vivid` included), `.size('xs'…'lg')`; `.shape('circle')`
  is the square icon-only button, `.shape('pill')` the wide one.
- **Paired colours.** `.colors(fill, label)` is Bloom's `colors` from two
  colour roles (or roles at an opacity), painted from the theme; the geometry
  stays the button's. `.inverted()` swaps the theme's background and
  foreground; `.linkTone('primary' | 'secondary' | 'text')` is a plain link's
  ink; `.density('default' | 'compact')` its geometry over the nearest scope's.
  `.shape('fit')` is Bloom's `grow`: an icon button that grows with its
  content, never narrower than it is tall.
- **Groups.** `ButtonGroup(...buttons)` is Bloom's `Button.Group`:
  `.axis('vertical')` stacks them, `.size()` sizes every one, `.dividers(false)`
  drops the hairlines, `.separated()` gaps the segments, `.label()` names it.
- **State.** `.loading()`, `.disabled()`, `.pressed(on)` (a toggle,
  announced as pressed), `.link(href)` (an anchor on the web, opened on
  native), `.lines(n)`, `.underline()` (at rest).
  `.accessibilityState({ expanded })` on a button is Bloom's `aria-expanded`
  (a menu trigger's open state; Bloom writes the button's own accessibility
  state).
- **Views beside the label.** With a `Text` label, a view before it (that is
  no `Icon`) is Bloom's `leading`, one after it its `trailing`, drawn as they
  are. A plain `Icon` child is the button's glyph (`leadingIcon`): Bloom
  paints it in its ink. A bare glyph as its content, `Button(Group(Icon(X)))`
  with no ink of its own (a `.size()` at most), is Bloom's render-function
  children, `(color) => <X fill={color} />`: drawn in the ink Bloom hands it
  (its tone's, `.foreground()`'s, under the pointer `.hoverForeground()`'s,
  disabled its muted one) with no glyph box of its own, as a compose
  toolbar's controls draw theirs (on the web the `leadingIcon` sits in Bloom's
  20pt glyph box; the render function's glyph does not). A circle or pill whose
  content is one view (`Button(Group(busy ? Loading().style('inline').size('sm') :
  Icon(X)))`, a spinner in the glyph's place while it works) is Bloom's icon
  button too (`iconOnly`), so it keeps its square.
- **Inks and spinner.** `.foreground(role)` is the button's ink at rest
  (Bloom's `color`) and `.hoverForeground(role)` under the pointer
  (`hoverColor`), each painted from a colour role. `.loadingIndicator(null)`
  draws no spinner while loading (the glyph or label stays); a view is drawn
  in its place.
- **Glyph and press.** `.iconSize(size)` is the glyph's size over its size's
  own (an icon rung, `xl` is 28, or a numeric size), Bloom's `iconSize`.
  `.stopPropagation()` keeps its press from reaching a pressable around it on
  the web (a button in a pressable row); native presses never bubble.
- **On glass** it keeps its own press, ink and name; the glass draws its fill
  (see [Glass](#glass)).
- **Glass styles.** `.style('glass')` and `.style('glassProminent')` are
  SwiftUI's glass button styles, the way to put a button on glass:

  ```ts
  Button(Text('Done')).style('glassProminent')                  // the key action: one per bar
  Button(Icon(RiShareLine)).label('Share').style('glass')
  HStack(a, b, c).buttonStyle('glass')                          // through the environment
  ```

  Each is the chain it stands for: `glass` is `.style('subtle').tint('neutral')`
  on `.glass()`, `glassProminent` `.style('solid').tint('accent')` on `.glass()`,
  whose untinted glass takes the button's fill. A `.tint()` the chain names
  wins, and a `.glass()` it names (`clear`, a tint) is the glass instead. A
  button on glass is Apple's size, the 44pt `lg` rung (Bloom's
  `FLOATING_CONTROL_SIZE`, measured on iOS 26 and 27), whatever size its
  chain or its environment names: the control decides it, never its caller.

  | | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `glass` | the button on the lens | on Liquid Glass, interactive; in the system bar a plain bar button | on Surface |
  | `glassProminent` | on the lens tinted with its fill | on Liquid Glass tinted with its fill; in the system bar `variant: 'prominent'` | on Surface with its fill |

Not in the language: a brand fill that is no colour role (a raw hex in
`colors`), the state colours over artwork (`color`, `hoverColor`…), which are
raw values with no token. A group's `material="lens"` is `.glass()` on the
`ButtonGroup`: the lens where the device has one, its shared Surface
elsewhere (the group's material has no tint or clear variant).

## Avatars

```ts
Avatar(user.avatar, { name: user.name, variant: 'thumb' }).size('lg').presence('online')
Avatar(null, { name: 'Ana Lima' }).tint('lime').shape('squircle')
Avatar(user.avatar).badge(Badge(unread).tint('danger'))
AvatarGroup(members).layout('row').max(4).total(count).initials()
```

`Avatar(source, options?)` is Bloom's `Avatar`: the image, else the account's
character, else initials on a tinted disc. The options are its data: `name`,
`initials`, `account`, `character`, `fallbackSource` and `variant` (the
rendition of a file id).

- `.size()` is an avatar rung or an app token (`defineTokens({ avatarSize: {
  feed: 48 } })`); with none, Bloom's 40.
- `.tint('neutral' | 'blue' | 'lime' | 'pink')` the initials disc;
  `.shape(name)` one of Bloom's silhouettes (`circle`, `squircle` and the 34
  named shapes); the image and the fallback keep it.
- Badges: `.verified()` (Bloom's mark), `.live()` (the ring and the LIVE pill,
  `.liveBadge(false)` for the ring alone), and any `Badge` attached with
  `.badge(Badge(…))`.
- Presence: `.presence('online' | 'idle' | 'busy' | 'offline')` is Bloom's
  `AvatarPresence`, the dot sized from the avatar.
- Motion: `.motion('auto' | 'always' | 'never')` is Bloom's `motion`, whether
  its character moves (an `AvatarGroup`'s, each member's); reduced motion always
  wins. An app's avatar that forwards every prop to Bloom's is a `view()`
  returning `Avatar(…)` with its policy's `.motion()`, which its callers chain
  their modifiers on.
- `.cornerRadius(radius)` rounds the avatar's own corners (Bloom's Avatar
  `style.borderRadius`, a corner token: `'md'` is 12): a rounded square no
  shape name draws (a list's cover). `.corner()` is a box around it.
- `.label(name)` is its `alt`; `.onPress` its own press.

`AvatarGroup(items)` takes Bloom's `AvatarGroupItem`s: `.layout('stack' |
'row' | 'cluster' | 'conversation')`, `.size()`, `.shape()`, `.max(n)`,
`.total(n)`, `.spacing(space)` (a row's gap), `.overflow('count' |
'truncate')`, `.initials()` (tinted initials instead of the neutral
placeholder), `.onPressItem(fn)`, `.hoverCard()`, and `.label()` to name it.
`.ringColor(color)` is the ring each face is cut out of its neighbours by
(Bloom's `ringColor`): a role painted from the theme (`'background'`, the
colour behind the group) or an app colour. `.variant(name)` is the rendition
the members' images load (`'thumb'` with none). `.itemAction((item, index) =>
view)` is Bloom's `renderItemAction`: a view under the member's name in its
hover card on the web (a follow button), drawn as a tree of its own.

## Small pieces

- **`Badge(content?)`**: `.style('solid' | 'subtle' | 'outlined' | 'onMedia')`,
  `.tint(tone)`, `.color(accentTone)` (Bloom's `color`: `default` `primary`
  `vivid` `secondary` `tertiary` `success` `warning` `error` `info`, its tone
  when no `.tint()` is set), `.size()` (the counters `xs`…`lg`, the labels `label-small`
  and `label-medium`), `.icon(X)`, `.dot()`, `.max(n)`, and `.placement()` when
  attached. `view.badge(Badge(…))` attaches it to any view.
- **`IconCircle(icon)`**: an icon on Bloom's tinted disc; `.size()` an icon rung.
- **`Loading(Text(…)?)`**: `.style('spinner' | 'top' | 'inline')`, `.size()`,
  `.tint(tone)` (or an app's own colour, Bloom's `color` as written: a white
  spinner over a photo); the `Text` is what it says, `.label()` names it as a progress bar.
  Its box modifiers (padding, frame, fill, corner, margin, flex) are its
  `style`, as an app writes them.
- **`SpinnerIcon()`**: Bloom's `SpinnerIcon` (`@oxy.so/bloom/loading`), the
  loader's morphing glyph alone. `.size()` is its side (an icon rung, a numeric
  side such as `'18'`, or an app token; Bloom's 26 by default), `.tint(color)`
  its `color`: a role painted from the theme, or an app colour as written
  (white over a video, the same in both schemes); its box modifiers are its
  `style`.
- **`Skeleton(shape)`**: `text`, `circle`, `pill` or `box`. Its frame is its
  size (a circle's and a pill's height, a box's width and height), a box's
  corner its radius, a text line's height its font's. It takes nothing else
  of a chain. `SkeletonGroup(...)` is `Skeleton.Region`, named with `.label()`.
- **`.pressScale()`**: a pressable view shrinks under the finger: its host is
  Bloom's `PressableScale`.
- **`.pressFade(opacity?)`**: a pressable view fades while pressed, as React
  Native's `TouchableOpacity` fades it, because that is its host: to the
  opacity token on touch (`'70'` is `activeOpacity={0.7}`; without one,
  `TouchableOpacity`'s own 0.2) and back on release, with
  `TouchableOpacity`'s own timing and curve on each platform (Animated on
  Android and iOS, react-native-web's opacity transition on the web). The
  view's layout, fill and accessibility props are the host's, as on a
  `Pressable`. It is press feedback, as `.pressScale()` is: a view takes one
  of them. Its focus is `TouchableOpacity`'s own: with `.onPress(undefined)`
  it is out of the focus order on Android and iOS and focusable on the web,
  as the `TouchableOpacity` an app writes (a `Pressable` without a press is
  out of it everywhere).
- **`.hoverHighlight(active?)`** on a stack: Bloom's `SubtleHover` wash behind
  it. With no argument it answers the pointer (the web; the stack becomes its
  hover group); with `active`, it is a coordinated highlight on every platform.

A pressable host is focusable by default, so the language writes `focusable`
only for a press modifier with no handler (`focusable={false}`).

## Inputs

```ts
const [name, setName] = useState('');
const [notify, setNotify] = useState(true);
// password, email, bio, terms, plan, range, country, query and filters are bindings too.
VStack(
  Field('Name', TextField([name, setName], 'Name').placeholder('Ada Lovelace')).required(),
  SecureField(password, 'Password').revealable().contentType('current-password'),
  TextField(email, 'Email').keyboard('email-address').autocapitalize('none').submitLabel('next'),
  TextEditor(bio, 'Bio').description('Shown on your profile').maxLength(280).showCount(),
  HStack(Text('Notifications'), Spacer(), Toggle([notify, setNotify]).label('Notifications')),
  Checkbox(terms, 'I accept the terms').description('You can read them any time.'),
  RadioGroup(plan, Option('free', 'Free'), Option('pro', 'Pro').description('For creators')).label('Plan'),
  Segmented(range, Option('day', 'Day'), Option('week', 'Week')).label('Range'),
  Picker(country, countries.map((c) => Option(c.code, c.name))).placeholder('Country').label('Country'),
  Search(query).placeholder('Search posts').tokens(filters),
)
```

**Bindings.** An input takes a `Binding<T>`: a value and the function that
changes it, `readonly [value, setValue]`, which is what `useState` returns, so
`Toggle(useState(false))` works and `TextField([draft.title, setTitle], 'Title')`
binds a store's value. The screen owns the value; the control reads it and
writes it. It is the one mechanism for every input (SwiftUI's `$value`); a
`Picker` takes a selection that may be `undefined` until something is chosen.

**A node's other editable values.** A node's main value is its constructor's
binding (`TextField(text)`, `NewGroupForm(name)`). Every other value it edits
is a modifier named after that value, taking a `Binding` too:
`.presented([open, setOpen])` (Bloom's `open`), `.query([text, setText])`,
`.expanded([open, setOpen])`, `.sorting([sort, setSort])` (Bloom's `sort`),
`.starred([starred, setStarred])`, `.month([month, setMonth])`… Elsewhere the
modifier is Bloom's own name for the value (`activeIndex`, `range`, `tab`,
`muted`), its setter the matching `on…Change`. One
not chained leaves the value to Bloom (uncontrolled), as an app that passes
neither; there is no handler alone, so a screen that wants to hear a change
owns the value. A value Bloom requires (a place picker's `.query()`, a pitch
card's selections) is still a modifier, said where the node is. Pull to
refresh is a binding as well: `.refreshable([refreshing, setRefreshing])`, a
pull writing `true`; or, where a query drives it, its value and action,
`.refreshable({ refreshing: isRefetching, onRefresh: refetch })` (React
Native's names), which writes nothing back. Each binding's type is the value Bloom holds
(`Bound<ReviewCardProps, 'expanded'>` is `Binding<boolean>`), and the
spec lists each modifier on the nodes that edit it, so the compiler rejects a
binding on a node that has no such value.

| Node | Bloom | Modifiers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `TextField(text, label)`, `SecureField(text, label)` | `TextFieldInput` in its `TextField` shell (`TextFieldIcon`, `TextFieldPrefix`, `TextFieldSuffix`, `TextFieldGhost`) | `.placeholder` (`null`: none, not even the label Bloom draws in its place otherwise), `.size`, `.disabled` (the shell and its input: what `<TextField disabled>` around a plain input draws too, since the input reads its shell's state), `.invalid`, `.floatingLabel()`, `.revealable()` (secure), `.icon(X)`, `.trailingIcon(X)`, `.prefix(text)`, `.suffix(text)`, `.completion(rest)`, `.focused(binding)`, `.keyboard`, `.autocapitalize`, `.autocorrect`, `.contentType` (autofill), `.submitLabel`, `.onSubmit`, `.autoFocus()`, `.maxLength`, `.multiline()`, `.editable(false)` (shown and selectable, no typing), `.inputMode` (the kind of text it takes: React Native's `inputMode`, the web's `inputmode`), `.inputRef(ref)` (its `TextInput`'s ref, to focus it or move its caret; `.ref()` is its box's), `.onSelectionChange(fn)` (React Native's, handed its event) |
| `TextEditor(text, label?)` | `Textarea` | `.placeholder`, `.description` (its hint), `.size`, `.disabled`, `.invalid`, `.required`, `.rows`, `.autoResize(maxRows?)`, `.resize`, `.maxLength`, `.showCount()`, `.focused(binding)`, `.label(name)` (its `accessibilityLabel`, beside the visible `label`), the keyboard modifiers, `.inputMode`, `.inputRef(ref)`, `.onSelectionChange(fn)` |
| `Field(label, ...content)` | `Field` | `.description`, `.error`, `.required`, `.disabled`: the label names its control, the error marks it invalid; `.multiple()` for a field of several controls (chips, two inputs), whose label names the group (Bloom's `multiple`) |
| `Label(text)` | `Label` | `.size('xs' \| 'sm' \| 'md')`, `.required`, `.disabled` |
| `Toggle(isOn)` | `Switch` | `.size`, `.tint(tone)`, `.disabled`; `.label()` names it (a switch draws no words) |
| `Checkbox(isOn, label?)` | `Checkbox` | `.description`, `.size`, `.tint`, `.disabled`, `.readOnly()`, `.indeterminate()` |
| `RadioGroup(selection, ...options)` | `RadioGroup` | `.style('default' \| 'card' \| 'chip' \| 'swatch')`, `.size`, `.tint`, `.disabled`; `.label()` names the group |
| `Segmented(selection, ...options)` | `SegmentedControl` with its `Item` and `ItemText` | `.style('solid' \| 'plain')`, `.size`, `.disabled`, `.tabs()` (a tab list, not a choice) |
| `Picker(selection, ...options)` | `Select`: its trigger (`SelectValue`, `SelectIcon`) and its list (`SelectItem` with its indicator and text) | `.placeholder`, `.size`, `.disabled`, `.presented(isOpen)` (its list open through a binding: Bloom's controlled `open`); `.label()` names it |
| `Option(value, label)` | an option of the three above | `.description` (a radio's), `.disabled`, `.testID`, `.leadingView(...)` (a picker row's mark), `.tint(role)` and `.foreground(role)` (a swatch's colour and its chosen dot) |
| `OptionGroup(label, ...options)` | a picker's `SelectGroup` with its `SelectLabel`, heading its first row | none |
| `Search(text)` | `Search` | `.placeholder`, `.size`, `.disabled`, `.editable(false)` (shown, taking no typing: React Native's `editable`), `.collapsible('auto'?)` (a button that opens the field), `.presented(isOpen)`, `.tokens(tokens)` (removing a token writes the binding), `.trigger(Button)`, `.trailing(...)`, `.focused(binding)`, `.submitLabel`, `.onSubmit`, `.onClearText(action)` (the clear button's press: the screen empties its text, and its tokens), `.maxLength(n)` (the most characters it takes), the keyboard modifiers, `.inputMode`, `.inputRef(ref)` (the `TextInput` Bloom's Search hands its `ref`), `.onSelectionChange(fn)` (its caret, as a mention or hashtag picker reads it) |

- **The box.** A field's box modifiers (`frame`, `padding`…) are its shell's
  style, as an app writes `<TextField style>`; a picker's are its trigger's;
  a `Search`'s are its `containerStyle` (its outer view), with no view around it.
- **Names.** `.label()` is the control's accessible name (a `TextField`'s
  is its constructor's `label`). Inside a `Field` the field's label names it.
- **Focus.** `.focused(binding)` is the input's focus as a binding (SwiftUI's
  `@FocusState`): focusing or leaving the input writes it, and setting it
  focuses or blurs the input.
- **Glyphs and completions.** `.icon(X)` sits before the text and
  `.trailingIcon(X)` after it (after a suffix); `.completion(rest)` draws the
  rest of a suggested text faintly after what is typed (`TextFieldGhost`).
- **Swatches.** `RadioGroup(...).style('swatch')` draws a disc per option in
  its `.tint(role)` (the accent without one); `.foreground(role)` is the chosen
  disc's dot. The colours are roles, painted from the theme as an app reads them.
- **Picker rows.** `OptionGroup('Fruit', ...)` heads its first row, as Bloom's
  grouped select does; an option's `.leadingView(view)` is its row's mark and
  the trigger's while it is chosen.
- **Search.** `.trigger(Button(...))` reads the collapsed search's button from a
  `Button` node: its style, tint, size and test id (Search owns its glyph and
  name). `.trailing(view)` is an action at the field's end (a voice search).

## Glass

SwiftUI's `glassEffect` materials, drawn by each platform's own glass (below),
and by Bloom's `Surface` where the platform has none. `regular` and `clear` are
exported values; `.tint(role, opacity?)` returns a tinted copy.

| Chain | On Surface (the web, and native without the platform's glass) |
| --- | --- |
| `.glass()` = `.glass(regular)` | Surface with its own fill |
| `.glass(regular.tint('primary'))` | `fill` = the role's colour (the web paints an opaque fill at 72%, as Surface always does) |
| `.glass(regular.tint('primary', 'medium'))` | `fill` = the role at the named opacity |
| `.glass(clear)` | `sheen={false}`, `fill` = `surface` at `subtle` |
| `.glass(clear.tint('error'))` | `sheen={false}`, `fill` = the role at `subtle` (or the opacity given) |

Opacities are tokens (`glassOpacity` in the spec): `subtle` 0.24, `dim` 0.35
(Apple's dimming layer: `clear.dimmed()` is `clear.tint('black', 'dim')`), `medium` 0.48,
`strong` 0.72 (`strong` is Surface's own web tint, `SURFACE_TINT_OPACITY`).
The fill is Bloom's `withAlpha` of the role's colour, as an app writes it:
Surface computes its material from its fill, and a `color-mix(…)` hid the
opacity from it (the web drew a `subtle` or `medium` tint at 72%, which the
Chrome check now catches: it compares a Surface's `::before` and `::after`,
where its material is drawn). A web paint that is a CSS variable (Bloom's
prepaint) is the one the browser mixes, with `color-mix(…)`.

Each platform draws the glass itself (`src/platform/glass-host.*`), and Surface
is what is left where it cannot:

- **Android and OxyOS:** the lens engine (`BloomLensMenu` with no rows, from
  `@oxy.so/bloom-lens`), on Android 13+ in a binary that has the module. The
  material frosts as Liquid Glass does: what is behind it is blurred slightly
  and bent at its edges. Its frost is the spec's `lensBlur`, the engine's blur
  radius in dp (`regular` 5, about 3 dp of sigma; `clear` 2), and a menu that
  grows out of it frosts more (`lensMenuBlur`, 24); Bloom's own lens controls
  take the same values (`LENS_FROST`). The tint is the role at the named
  opacity, else the engine's own. The lens belongs to the fixed layer (a header, a bar,
  a floating button): the window draws it, so it does not follow a scroll
  view's overscroll, and each one is drawn every frame.
- **iOS:** Liquid Glass (`expo-glass-effect`'s `GlassView`, iOS 26), its style
  the spec's `iosStyle` (the system frosts `regular` and `clear` itself), the
  tint as a colour with the token's opacity, interactive under a control;
  before iOS 26, `expo-blur`'s system material under the tint (the spec's
  `iosFallback`: thin for `regular`, ultra-thin for `clear`). Both are
  optional.
- **Web:** Surface, Bloom's frosted material with the measured saturation and
  contrast under the tint, frosted as the glass is (Surface's `frost`, the
  spec's `webBlur`: `blur(3px)` for `regular`, `blur(1.5px)` for `clear`,
  against Surface's own 24px). The web has no glass.

A glass's variants (its frame, border, corner and fill; see
[Variants](#variants)) reach each platform's glass as its props: the lens's
`cornerRadius` and tint, Liquid Glass's radius and `tintColor`, Surface's
`radius` and `fill`. Not verified on a device: they are held by unit tests
on the lens and iOS stand-ins, and by parity and Chrome on Surface.

**A control on glass.** A `Button` or `FAB` on glass keeps its own press,
ink, size and name (Bloom's `material="shared"`); the glass draws its fill.
An untinted glass under a solid control takes the control's fill (the spec's
`toneRole`: `accent` is `primary`, `action` is `tertiary`…), so the ink still
reads. It is tinted glass, as Apple's `glassProminent` is: on the lens at
Bloom's solid lens control's 0.8, so the glass still shows through the fill;
the system's tinted Liquid Glass on iOS (the fill at 0.8 over the blur before
iOS 26); Bloom's surface tint (72%) over the frost on the web. A `FAB` is always on glass,
tinted with the accent (the theme's primary, its glyph the on-primary colour)
unless it names a `.tint()`. `.shape('circle' | 'rounded' | 'squircle')` is its
silhouette (Bloom `Fab`'s `shape`): a squircle by default (a square whose corner
is 28% of its diameter, continuous), the same square with a round corner
(`rounded`), or a `circle`; its glass takes that corner at the FAB's diameter,
so the lens, its press and a menu's morph follow the shape. The lens draws every corner continuous, so the two squares
read alike on Android and OxyOS.

**Interactive and identity glass.** `regular.interactive(on?)` says whether the
glass answers touch (SwiftUI's `Glass.interactive`), and `identity` is no glass
at all (SwiftUI's `Glass.identity`): the chain keeps its shape, so a value can
turn the glass off (`.glass(editing ? regular : identity)`).

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `interactive()` unsaid | the base springs under a press | interactive under a press or a control | Surface |
| `interactive(false)` | the engine's jelly off (`jelly: 0`) | `GlassView isInteractive={false}` | Surface (it never reacts) |
| `interactive(true)` | the base springs | `isInteractive` | Surface; press feedback is `.pressScale()` |
| `identity` | no lens | no `GlassView` | no Surface |

An unsaid `interactive` keeps each platform's default, so no existing glass
changes; saying it is what makes the platforms agree.

### Glass groups

`GlassGroup(...)` is SwiftUI's `GlassEffectContainer`: the glasses inside it
render together. Apple's rule is to put nearby glass in one container, and the
compiler warns when two glasses sit side by side in a stack outside one (see
[Liquid Glass rules](#liquid-glass-rules)).

```ts
const ns = useNamespace();
GlassGroup(
  HStack(
    Button(Icon(PencilIcon)).label('Draw').glass().glassID('pencil', ns),
    erasing ? Button(Icon(EraserIcon)).label('Erase').glass().glassID('eraser', ns) : null,
  ).spacing('xl'),
)
  .blend('xl')
  .animation('expand', erasing)
Badge(count).glass().glassID('badge', ns).glassTransition('materialize')
```

- **`.blend(space)`** is the container's spacing: glasses closer than it blend
  into one shape. (A stack's gap stays `.spacing()`; on a composer loader
  `.blend()` is its blend mode, a token set of its own.)
- **`.glassID(id, ns)`** is a glass's identity in its group: one that arrives
  grows out of where the glass with its id was, and one that leaves goes back
  into it. **`.glassTransition('matchedGeometry' | 'materialize')`** chooses:
  `matchedGeometry` (the default) is the motion engine's matched geometry with
  that id, `materialize` the engine's fade where the glass is (a
  `.transition()` or `.matchedGeometry()` the chain names itself wins).
- **`.glassUnion(id, ns)`**: the glasses of one group with the same id are one
  shape (see the table for what each platform draws).

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `GlassGroup` | one lens base drawn as the glasses inside it, each its own shape where its view is; a menu from anything in it grows out of the whole group | UIKit's glass container (`expo-glass-effect`'s `GlassContainer spacing={blend}`) around the `GlassView`s | a view; each glass is its own `Surface` |
| a glass inside | no lens of its own (`useInLensBase`): the base draws its shape, found by its view's `nativeID` | its `GlassView` | its `Surface` |
| `.blend()` | the engine's smooth union: shapes closer than it flow together | the container's `spacing` | not read |
| `.glassUnion()` | the glasses with that id are one shape around them all | the container's merge within `blend` | each `Surface` on its own (decided: see below) |
| `.glassID()` | the shape's key: the glass that takes the id over takes the shape there on a spring, and the content keeps the motion engine's matched geometry | SwiftUI's own `glassEffectID` for a group of glass icon buttons (below); else the motion engine's matched geometry | matched geometry (the Web Animations API) |

**iOS's own morph (not verified).** A group that is one stack of glass icon
buttons, each a glyph with an SF Symbol, a `.label()`, `.glass()` and
`.glassID()`, is drawn by SwiftUI through `@expo/ui`: a `GlassEffectContainer`
(its spacing the blend) in a `Namespace`, each button a SwiftUI button with
`glassEffect` and `glassEffectId(id, namespace)`, and a spring `animation` on
the group keyed by its ids, so a button that arrives grows out of its
neighbour as Apple's `glassEffectID` does. Any other group stays UIKit's
container around Bloom's views.

**Android: a group draws its glasses.** The lens base of a group is given
its glasses as members (`LensBase members blend`): each glass marks its view
with its key (`nativeID`: its `glassID`, its `glassUnion`, or one of its own),
and the engine finds and measures those views before each frame and draws
each as a shape of one field, blended within `blend` by the smooth union the
menu's morph uses. A view's own motion moves its glass with it; what a view
cannot do the engine does on a spring: a glass that arrives grows out of the
nearest one, one that leaves goes back into it (`materialize`: in place), and
an id that another glass takes over takes its shape there. The field holds
eight shapes; a group with more draws its first eight.

**Decided: a union is the platform's merge.** `.glassUnion()` draws no shape
of its own. On Android the engine draws the union's glasses as one shape; on
iOS UIKit's container merges glasses within `blend`; on the web each glass is
its own `Surface`. Drawing a union as one glass around its members would replace each
member's own glass (and the glass a glass button style adds) with one around
them, and tell the members they sit on it. The island environment (a `.blur()`
whose controls share its material) is the nearest mechanism, but it marks
Bloom's island, and a glass ends it by design: reusing it would give it a
second meaning, and rewriting the members inside a group is a second path for
a glass. Neither is clean, so the union stays the platform's merge.

The web's glass is a blurred `Surface` with no refraction and no shared
sampling, so shapes there never blend; that is Bloom's measured rule (no SVG
filters, masks or clip paths on a material host), and the owner's direction
that the web may differ.

### Liquid Glass rules

Apple's Liquid Glass guidance, as the compiler holds it within one expression
(a chain inside another's children; a `view()`'s own body is checked where it
is written):

| Rule | Apple | Compiler |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No glass on glass | "avoid overcrowding or layering Liquid Glass elements on top of each other" | **error**: a glass (`.glass()` other than `identity`, a glass button style, a `FAB`) in another glass's children |
| Glass belongs to the navigation layer | "Don't use Liquid Glass in the content layer" | **warning**: a glass in the children of a `ScrollView`, `ScrollArea`, `ScrollSurface`, `List`, `VirtualList` or `Card` |
| Nearby glass shares one container | "use `GlassEffectContainer` when applying Liquid Glass effects on multiple views" | **warning**: two or more glasses side by side in a stack outside a `GlassGroup` |
| Every icon-only control has a label | "Provide an accessibility label for every icon" | **error**: a `Button` or `FAB` whose content is only `Icon`s, with no `.label()` |
| At most three toolbar groups | "aim for a maximum of three" groups | **warning**: more than three `ToolbarGroup`s in one `.toolbar()` |
| Text actions apart from icon actions | "keep actions with text labels separate" | **warning**: a `ToolbarGroup` with both a text `Button` and an icon-only one |
| Clear glass over bright media is dimmed | `Glass.clear`: "add a dimming layer … when the underlying content is bright" | **warning**: `.glass(clear)` with neither a tint nor `.dimmed()` (`clear.dimmed()` is black at the `dim` opacity, 35%, under the glass's content) |
| One tinted control per group | "Refrain from adding color to the background of multiple controls"; "Only specify one primary action" | **warning**: two or more tinted glasses (`.tint()`, `glassProminent`) in one `GlassGroup`, `ToolbarGroup` or `.toolbar()` |
| Limit the glass on screen | "Limit the use of Liquid Glass effects onscreen at the same time" | **warning**: more than 12 glasses in one module, and a glass in a `.map()` callback (one per item; `.inContent()` says it is on purpose). The budget sits well under the 60 lenses Bloom measured at 21% janky frames on a Pixel 8a |

A warning is printed (`warning[bloom]`, `console.warn` in Metro and Jest) and
the module compiles; an error fails it, as every other check does.

**The one exception to the navigation layer** is a row's control that is glass
on purpose (a feed's ⋯ is not: it is a bare trigger, see Menu). A glass that is
in the content on purpose says so with `.inContent()`, which silences the
warning:

```ts
Menu(Button(Icon(RiMoreFill)).label('More').glass().inContent(), ...rows)
```

Android also holds glass on glass at run time: nothing inside a lens base
draws a lens of its own.

## Platform components

One API; each platform draws it with its own component (`src/platform/`,
`*.android.tsx`, `*.ios.tsx`, `*.web.tsx`; OxyOS takes the Android files).

```ts
Header('Notifications')
  .titleDisplay('large')
  .toolbar(
    Button(Icon(RiSearchLine)).style('subtle').tint('neutral').shape('circle').label('Search'),
    Menu(
      Button(Icon(RiMoreFill)).style('plain').shape('circle').label('More'),
      Item('Copy link').icon(RiLinkM).onPress(copy),
      Submenu('Share', Item('Copy'), Item('Share to…')).icon(RiShareLine),
      Section(Item('Delete').icon(RiDeleteBinLine).destructive().onPress(remove)).title('Danger'),
    ),
  )

BottomBar(Tab('Home', RiHome5Line).id('home'), Tab('Search', RiSearchLine).id('search'))
  .selection([page, setPage])
  .action(FAB(Icon(RiQuillPenLine)).label('Compose').onPress(compose))
```

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Menu` | the lens menu | the system menu (SwiftUI `Menu` through `@expo/ui`) | Bloom `DropdownMenu` |
| `Button` | Bloom `Button`; on glass, on the lens | Bloom `Button`; on glass, on Liquid Glass | Bloom `Button`; on glass, on Surface |
| `FAB` | Bloom `Fab` on the lens | Bloom `Fab` on Liquid Glass | Bloom `Fab` on Surface; `.glass(identity)`: Bloom's solid `Fab` everywhere, as an app's `<Fab>` draws it |
| `Header` | Bloom `PageHeader` (a floating header's controls take the lens) | the navigation bar (expo-router's native `Stack`, `Stack.Toolbar`) | Bloom `PageHeader` |
| `BottomBar` | Bloom `BottomBar` (its capsule on the lens) | Bloom `BottomBar` (its capsule on the system blur); an app's tabs are the system tab bar (see [Navigation](#navigation)) | Bloom `BottomBar` |

**Menu.** `Menu(trigger, ...rows)`: `Item` (`.icon`, `.iconSize`, `.iconForeground`,
`.subtitle`, `.destructive`, `.disabled`, `.checked`, `.keepOpen`, `.onPress`),
`Submenu` (one level; `.icon`, `.iconSize`, `.iconForeground`, `.disabled`), `Section` (`.title`, set apart from the rows beside it;
`.selection()`, a section of choices, below) and `Divider`. A row's glyph is a 20pt Bloom icon in the menu's quiet ink (the
negative ink for a destructive row; the row's own ink on the lens menu). A row
sizes and inks its own with `.iconSize(size)` (an icon rung or a numeric size,
`'22'`) and `.iconForeground(role)`, over those: Bloom's dropdown, context and
menubar menus and the lens menu (Android, OxyOS) draw them; the system menu on
iOS draws its SF Symbol at its own size and ink. A `Submenu` takes the same two
for its own glyph; Bloom's menus draw them, and the lens menu's submenus draw
no glyph. `.menuStyle('dropdown')` draws Bloom's own DropdownMenu on every
platform (its panel and rows, as an app's `<DropdownMenu>` draws them) rather
than the platform's menu; `platform` is the default. Rows built from hooks, or costly to build, go in one
`menu()` content, whose body runs only while the menu is open:

```ts
const PostMenu = menu(function PostMenu({ post }: { post: Post }) {
  const actions = usePostActions(post);
  return actions.map((action) => Item(action.label).icon(action.icon).onPress(action.run));
});
Menu(MORE, PostMenu({ post }))
```

- *Android:* the lens menu is the upstream app's menu component itself
  (`@oxy.so/bloom-lens`: its `Menu`, `Menu.Trigger` and `Menu.Content`,
  copied verbatim by `sync-from-upstream.sh`), and this host only adapts the
  `Menu` to it. The glass of every Bloom control is one **lens base**, the
  upstream app's glass trigger (`LensBase` in `@oxy.so/bloom-lens`): a button
  on glass, a `ButtonGroup` on glass, a header's action group, the bottom bar.
  A menu opened from anything inside a base is that base's, and the whole base
  becomes the menu and takes it back, as the upstream app's toolbar pill does
  (`absorb` over 0.48 s, the base's own config); a press anywhere on it springs
  the whole base, and nothing inside a base draws a lens of its own. A menu
  outside any base is a base of its own: on `.glass()` (the fixed layer) drawn
  by the window, with the glass's tint and frost; anywhere else (a feed row's
  ⋯) it is a bare trigger, as SwiftUI's `Menu` with a plain label and Apple's
  track-row ⋯ are: its base draws no lens (`lens={false}`), and the menu still
  grows out of its shape and goes back into it. The trigger's `.corner()` is the base's radius (a capsule
  without one). The trigger's press opens the menu natively;
  press-and-drag across the open panel, submenus, haptics and predictive back
  are the component's own. Rows given as they are (tagged, or with handlers)
  become its `Menu.Item`, `Menu.Submenu`, `Menu.Label` and `Menu.Separator`, a
  glyph a view in the row's ink (a submenu takes none; a disabled one is a
  disabled row), held by the base before it opens. A `menu()` content is built
  by the trigger's press: the base opens with its rows as they arrive
  (`openWithItems`) and lets them go once the menu has closed. Bloom gives only the colours (the panel's tint and the
  rows' ink from its menu palette, the theme's scheme) and the face (Inter);
  every other value is the upstream app's. A row's `.subtitle` is drawn only
  here. Without the lens, Bloom's menu.
- *iOS:* the system menu takes its rows before it opens and opens itself, so
  a `menu()` content (built only while open) is Bloom's menu there; rows given
  as they are (tagged, or with handlers) are the system's. Each row, submenu
  and toggle shows its icon's SF Symbol (see [Icons on iOS](#icons-on-ios)); an
  icon without one draws no glyph.

**Opened by the app.** `.presented([isOpen, setOpen])` opens the menu through
a binding, as SwiftUI's `isPresented`: setting `true` opens it as its trigger's
press does, and it sets `false` as it closes (`.presented([false, set])` keeps it
shut). Bloom's menus take it as `DropdownMenu`'s `open` and `onOpenChange`; the
lens menu opens and closes its base from it and reports its own opening and
closing; the system menu on iOS opens only from its trigger, so a presented
menu is Bloom's menu there.

**Choices.** A `Section` with a `.selection(binding)` is one choice among its
rows, as SwiftUI's `Picker` in a `Menu` is: each row is an `Item` whose
`.tag()` is its value (as a picker row's `tag`), marked while the binding holds
it, and choosing it sets the binding. `undefined` is no row chosen. Its rows are
its choices only: a row that is no tagged `Item` is refused, by the compiler
where it can see the row and by the runtime always.

```ts
Menu(
  trigger,
  Section(Item(t('Everyone')).tag('public'), Item(t('Private mention')).tag('direct'))
    .title(t('Who can see this post'))
    .selection([audience, setAudience]),
)
```

Bloom's menus (the dropdown, context and menubar menus) draw the title as a
label over a radio group (`DropdownMenuLabel`, then `DropdownMenuRadioGroup` of
`DropdownMenuRadioItem`s, each with its glyph, `.disabled()`, `.keepOpen()` and
`testID`), exactly as an app writes it; the lens menu (Android, OxyOS) a check
on the chosen row; the system menu on iOS SwiftUI's inline `Picker` in a
`Section` (a `UIMenu` of single selection), each choice a `Label` tagged with its
value, which dismisses as a whole: the menu stays open only when every choice
`.keepOpen()`s.

**Tagged rows.** A row can name its action instead of holding a handler, and
the menu takes what its tags do as an `actions()` view. The rows are plain data
a closed menu builds without a hook or a closure (so the system menu on iOS can
be given them); the body runs only when a row is chosen, in a component mounted
for that choice where the menu is placed, so it may call hooks. The choice
outlives the menu, so a menu that closes as it is chosen still runs it.

```ts
const PostActions = actions(function PostActions({ post }: { post: Post }) {
  const router = useRouter();
  return { open: () => router.push(`/p/${post.id}`), copy: () => copyLink(post) };
});
Menu(MORE, Item('Open').tag('open'), Item('Copy link').icon(RiLinkM).tag('copy'))
  .actions(PostActions({ post }))
```

Rows that only exist after a query (a submenu of the reader's lanes) stay a
`menu()` content, and on iOS that menu stays Bloom's.

### Icons on iOS

`spec/language.json`'s `sfSymbols` maps Bloom icons to SF Symbols, for iOS's
own components: the tab bar, the navigation bar's items and system menus.
`scripts/generate.mjs` checks both sides of every entry (a Bloom icon in
`src/icons/remix`, a symbol `sf-symbols-typescript` names) and writes the table
by icon component (`src/generated/sf-symbols.generated.ts`), which only
`*.ios.tsx` files import. An icon without an entry draws no glyph in a system
component; a toolbar button whose glyph has none stays Bloom's own view in the
bar.

**Header.** `Header(title)` with `.titleDisplay('large' | 'inline')` (large is
the ramp's `title-1-bold`), `.subtitle`, `.toolbar(...)`, `.toolbarLeading(...)`,
`.onBack(fn | null)`, `.scrollEdge('auto' | 'always' | 'none')` (Bloom's
scrim), `.scrollY` and `.presentation('floating' | 'bar' | 'navigation')`. A
title or subtitle may be a view (`Header(HStack(Avatar(a), Text(name)))`,
`.subtitle(Text(handle).foreground('muted'))`): Bloom's `PageHeader` draws it in
the title's place as it is, and on iOS's system bar a title view is UIKit's
(`headerTitle`; the system bar draws no subtitle). On
iOS, in a screen of a native stack, the header sets the screen's options and
its `Stack.Toolbar`: a `Button` whose glyph has an SF Symbol (or a text label)
is a bar button, a `Menu` of plain or tagged rows opened from one is a bar
menu, anything else Bloom's own view in the bar. The system back button pops
the stack (a custom `onBack` is not called; `null` hides it). Outside a native
stack (a tab page without a stack of its own, a panel), or with `.inPage()`,
Bloom's PageHeader. The PageHeader's own props are modifiers of the same
name: `.safeArea(on?)`, `.titleAlign('start' | 'center')`, `.accessory(view)`
(full-width chrome under the title row), `.tone('default' | 'onImage')`,
`.scrimColor(role)` (the opaque colour the scrim fades from, painted from the
theme, or an app's own colour as written, the same in both schemes), `.scrimFalloff('smooth' | 'linear')` and `.titleReveal('auto' |
'always' | 'onScroll' | 'onDock')`. The system bar has none of them: a header
that needs them on iOS in a native stack is `.inPage()`.

**Toolbar groups.** `ToolbarGroup(...items)` is SwiftUI's `ToolbarItemGroup`:
items that share one background. `ToolbarSpacer('fixed' | 'flexible')` is
`ToolbarSpacer`: a small gap between groups (Bloom's `sm`), or the free space.

```ts
Header('Landmark').toolbar(
  ToolbarGroup(ShareButton()),
  ToolbarSpacer('fixed'),
  ToolbarGroup(FavoriteButton(), CollectionsMenu()),
  ToolbarSpacer('fixed'),
  Button(Text('Done')).style('glassProminent'),
)
```

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ToolbarGroup` | one lens base: one lens over the group, and a menu from any item grows out of the whole group | in the system bar, its items side by side on the bar's shared background; in Bloom's `PageHeader`, one Liquid Glass capsule | one `Surface` capsule |
| `ToolbarSpacer` | a gap, or the free space | `Stack.Toolbar.Spacer` with the gap's width (a bar's spacer needs one) | a gap, or the free space |

A group is `HStack(...items).glass()`, so an item inside on glass of its own
is glass on glass (a compiler error). The compiler warns past three groups in
one toolbar, and when a group mixes text and icon buttons (Apple: keep actions
with text labels apart).

**BottomBar.** `BottomBar(...tabs)`, `Tab(label, icon)` with `.id`,
`.activeIcon` and `.badge`; the bar takes `.selection([id, setId])` (a
binding: Bloom's `value`, and its `onValueChange` a tab's press writes),
`.action(FAB(...))`, `.minimize` (Screen's collapse), `.progress` (a pager's
selection) and `.hide` (its presence). The system tab bar on iOS is a
navigator (expo-router's `NativeTabs`), not a view the bar could be: the
router's `TabView()` is it (see [Navigation](#navigation)).

## Navigation

`@oxy.so/bloom-language/router`, the only part of the language that imports
expo-router. expo-router's files stay the URL map, so deep links,
`+native-intent`, typed routes and the web fallback are untouched; the
language says what a layout file and a screen file render.

```ts
// app/(app)/_layout.tsx
export default layout(function App() {
  return AppLayout(Stack('(tabs)', Sheet('compose'), 'p/[id]/boost'))
    .primaryAction(RiQuillPenLine, t('sidebar.compose'), 'write')
    .bottomBar(signedIn)
    .shell((navigator) => ({ panelLayout: { panels: [{ id: 'main', role: 'main', content: navigator }] } }));
});

// app/(app)/(tabs)/_layout.tsx
export default layout(() => TabView());

// app/(app)/(tabs)/notifications/_layout.tsx: the page's own stack (its system bar on iOS)
export default layout(() => Stack());

// app/(app)/(tabs)/notifications/index.tsx
export default screen(function Notifications() {
  const [filters, setFilters] = useState(false);
  return Embed(<NotificationsList />)
    .title(t('notifications.title'))
    .toolbar(Button(Icon(RiSettings3Line)).label(t('settings')).onPress(openSettings))
    .onReselect(refresh)
    .sheet(filters, () => Filters(), { onDismiss: () => setFilters(false) });
});

// anywhere: through the navigator of the layout() or screen() it is drawn under
PostRow({ id }).onPress(navigate('/p/[id]', { id }))
```

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Stack(...routes)` | `ExperimentalStack` (predictive back), Bloom's scenes | expo-router's native `Stack` | the route in place (`Slot`): the document scrolls |
| `Sheet(name)` | pushed | the system page sheet (`presentation: 'modal'`) | the route in place |
| `TabView()` | Bloom `TabPagerLayout` (swiped) | the system tab bar (`NativeTabs`) | Bloom `TabPagerLayout` (routed in place) |
| `AppLayout` | `AppShell`; the bar is the language `BottomBar` of the pager's pages with the FAB, on the lens | `AppShell` without a bar; the primary destination is the prominent tab | `AppShell`; the same bar, on Bloom's material |
| `screen()` | Bloom `Screen` + `PageHeader` | Bloom `Screen` + the system navigation bar, in a native stack | Bloom `Screen` + `PageHeader` |

**`layout(body)`, `screen(body)`.** A route file's default export. The body
runs as the route's component, so it may call hooks; a screen a route renders
with props (a post's id), a gate's inner screen or a sheet's, is a `screen()`
whose body takes them. Both provide the
environment's navigator (Bloom's panel-aware router, so a deck panel's routes
push in that panel on the web), which `navigate()` presses go through.

`screen()` wraps the whole component: it draws the one Bloom `Screen`, and
each branch of its body (a loading, an error, the content) returns its content
with the chrome it shows. The Screen stays mounted from one branch to the
next, as React keeps one `<Screen>` that each branch of a component returns.

```ts
export default screen(function EditList() {
  const { list, loading, error } = useList();
  const header = Header(t('lists.edit')).backLabel(t('common.back')).inPage();
  if (loading) return ScrollView(Embed(<FormSkeleton />)).header(header);
  if (error) return ErrorState(error).header(header);
  return ScrollView(...list.members.map(MemberRow)).header(header);
});
```

**`AppLayout(navigator)`.** Bloom's `AppShell` around the navigator. The shell
draws its navigation from the `TabPagerProvider` above it (the sidebar's rows
and selection), which stays above the shell: bottom sheets and Bloom's native
portal outlet render above the route, so a provider mounted in the layout is
invisible to them. `.primaryAction(icon, label, destination)` is the sidebar's
first row and the bar's FAB, and on iOS the destination's tab, drawn on its own
surface (`prominent`, iOS 27); a handler in the destination's place is pressed
instead (a page's own action, chosen by the page in front). `.bottomBar(shown)` says whether the bar shows on
this route. The shell's own options (layout mode, panels, error boundaries)
stay the app's: `.shell(navigator => options)` places the navigator in the
panel it names.

On a device the app runs edge to edge: `AppLayout` gives the shell no
safe-area frame (`safeArea: false`), whatever `layout` the app's options name.
The pages own the device's edges, as Bloom's `Screen` and `PageHeader` do: each
screen's header keeps its controls below the status bar and the camera while
its paint and the scrolling content reach behind them (on iOS the system
navigation bar does the same). The bottom bar, the FAB and the reveal drawer
clear their own insets. A semantic `layout` (`feed`, `deck`) on a bare
`AppShell` frames the top instead and paints a band behind the status bar; an
app that wants that frame asks for it with `.shell(() => ({ safeArea: true }))`.
The web has no frame either way.

**`Stack(...routes)`, `Sheet(name)`.** Each name is a route of the layout's
directory, pushed over the ones before it; with none, the directory's routes as
expo-router finds them. A tab page that should have the system navigation bar
on iOS is a directory with a `Stack()` layout: it holds the page alone, and
everything opened from it is pushed over the tabs, in the app's stack. On
Android and OxyOS a stack inside a page of Bloom's pager is the page in place:
the pager's pages are not fragments, and a native stack in one throws
(react-native-screens: "Parent fragment providing view returned nullish
fragment", on a Pixel 8a).

**`screen()`'s chrome** is the screen modifiers on its body's root (any view
takes them, as SwiftUI's `navigationTitle`, `toolbar` and `sheet`; elsewhere
they draw nothing): `.title`, `.titleDisplay`, `.subtitle`, `.toolbar`,
`.toolbarLeading`, `.onBack`, `.backLabel`, `.headingLevel` (the web's
`aria-level`), `.header(presentation | false | node)` (a
`Header` node is the system navigation bar on iOS in a native stack, as the
composed header is; a `Header` with `.inPage()`, or any other node, is the
Screen's header in the page on every platform),
`.footer(content, { placement, keyboard })` (Bloom Screen's bottom bar),
`.onReselect` and `.sheet(open, content, { onDismiss, title })`, Bloom's
`Dialog` from the bottom and centred from `md`, its content built while open
and kept while it closes. The root's `.testID` names the Screen itself, not a
box around the content.

## Overlays and feedback

What a view presents is a modifier on it, as in SwiftUI: a binding
(`[value, setValue]`, what `useState` returns) says whether it shows, and
dismissing it sets the binding false.

```ts
const [confirming, setConfirming] = useState(false);
Button(Text('Delete')).onPress(() => setConfirming(true)).alert(
  [confirming, setConfirming],
  Alert('Delete post?').message('It cannot be undone.').confirmLabel('Delete').destructive().onConfirm(remove),
)
const sharing = useState(false);
Button(Text('Share')).onPress(() => sharing[1](true)).dialog(
  sharing,
  Dialog(Text(post.link)).title('Share').presentAt('bottom', { md: 'center' }).buttons([{ label: 'Done' }]),
)
// The popover's trigger is the view: pressing it opens the popover.
Button(Icon(RiInformationLine)).shape('circle').label('Details').popover(
  useState(false),
  Popover(Text(post.summary)).label('Details').side('bottom', 'end'),
)
ConnectionStatus()
toast.success('Saved')
```

| Chain | Draws |
| --- | --- |
| `.alert(isPresented, Alert(title))` | Bloom `AlertDialog` beside the view (`open`, `onOpenChange`) |
| `.dialog(isPresented, Dialog(...content))` | Bloom `Dialog` beside the view (`open`, `onClose`), its content the children |
| `Dialog(...content).presented(isPresented)` | the same Bloom `Dialog`, as a view of its own in place |
| `Dialog(...content).control(control)` | the same, opened and closed by Bloom's dialog control (`useDialogControl()`: `control.open()`, `control.close()`), for a dialog another view opens (a FAB): Bloom's `control` |
| `.popover(isPresented, Popover(...content))` | Bloom `Popover`: the view is its `PopoverTrigger asChild`, the content its `PopoverContent` |
| `.confirmationDialog(isPresented, ConfirmationDialog(title, ...rows))` | the lens menu grown out of the view (Android, OxyOS), SwiftUI's `confirmationDialog` (iOS), Bloom `Dialog` (the web) |
| `ConnectionStatus()` | Bloom `ConnectionStatusToasts` |
| `toast(…)` | Bloom's `toast` |

- **Alert.** `.message(text)` is the text under the title;
  `.confirmLabel()`, `.cancelLabel()` (Bloom's localized words without them),
  `.onConfirm(fn)`, `.onCancel(fn)` (the cancel button, Escape or the
  backdrop), `.destructive()`, `.hideCancel()` (an acknowledgement),
  `.defaultAction('confirm' | 'cancel')` (the initial focus and Enter),
  `.dismissible(false)`, `.icon(view)` (a view above the title: the
  application's icon), `.testID()`.
- **Dialog.** `.title()`, `.message()`, `.label()` (its name without a title),
  `.buttons([...])` (Bloom's `DialogAction`s: `label`, `onPress`, `color`,
  `disabled`, `loading`…), `.presentAt(base, tiers?)` (`center` `bottom` `left`
  `right` `start` `end`; `tiers` from each breakpoint up, so
  `.presentAt('bottom', { md: 'center', xl: 'end' })` is Bloom's
  `{ base: 'bottom', md: 'center', xl: 'end' }`), `.header(config)` (Bloom's
  dialog header: a collapsing title, its slots), `.footer(view)`,
  `.page(frame)` (Bloom's `frame`: a page of a dialog that navigates, by its
  key), `.frame({ width, maxWidth })` and `.padding(space)` (its
  `contentPadding`), `.sideGutter(space)` (its `minSideGutter`),
  `.maxHeightRatio(0.9)` (its height ceiling as a fraction of the window's),
  `.dismissOnBackdrop(false)`, `.scrollable()`, `.testID()`,
  `.onDismissed(action)` (Bloom's `onDismissed`: once the dialog has finished
  closing, its exit animation done, after its binding turned false; the place
  to unmount what presented it, as an app's `control.close(callback)` did). The widths apps
  use are numeric tokens (`.frame({ maxWidth: '560' })`); another is an app
  token (`defineTokens({ space: { sheet: 609 } })`).
- **Popover.** `.label()`, `.side(side, align?)` (`top` `bottom` `left`
  `right`; `start` `center` `end`), `.sideOffset(space)` (the gap to the view),
  `.alignOffset(space)` (a shift along the side, negatives included),
  `.frame({ minWidth, maxWidth, maxHeight })`, `.testID()`. Pressing the view opens it, as
  Bloom's trigger does, through the binding.
- **ConfirmationDialog.** SwiftUI's `confirmationDialog`, and Apple's rule
  that an action sheet "originates from the element that initiates the
  action": the rows are menu rows (`Item`, tagged with `.actions()` as a
  `Menu`'s, or with their handlers), `.message()` the text under the title,
  `.titleVisibility('automatic' | 'visible' | 'hidden')`, `.cancelLabel()` an
  explicit cancel row where the platform draws none of its own. Every row
  dismisses it, and dismissing it is its cancel.

  ```ts
  Button(Text('Delete')).style('subtle').tint('danger').onPress(() => setAsking(true))
    .confirmationDialog(
      [asking, setAsking],
      ConfirmationDialog('Delete this post?', Item('Delete').destructive().onPress(remove),
        Item('Archive instead').onPress(archive)).message('It cannot be undone.'),
    )
  ```

  | | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Drawn as | the lens menu, grown out of the view: the nearest lens base, or a base of its own around the view with no resting lens (`lens={false}`); the title and message are its headings | `@expo/ui`'s `ConfirmationDialog`, its trigger the view (`RNHostView`): an action sheet from it, a popover in regular width | Bloom `Dialog`, from the bottom and centred from `md`, its rows a column of actions (a destructive row in the destructive colour) |
  | Cancel | outside and back | the system's own row | the backdrop and Escape |
  | Without the platform's | Bloom's dialog | Bloom's dialog | |
- **Not views.** `Alert`, `Dialog` and `Popover` are what a view presents; the
  types keep them out of a stack. An alert and a dialog are drawn beside the
  view that presents them (each portals itself over the screen), as an app
  writes them next to the control that opens them; their content is in the
  view's environment.
- **A dialog as a view.** A component that is a dialog, opened by its caller,
  returns `Dialog(...).presented(isPresented)`: the binding handed in
  (`[open, () => onClose()]` from an `open` and `onClose` pair), the dialog drawn
  where the view is, by the same code `.dialog()` draws it with. `.presented()`
  ends the chain (it is a view, with no modifier of its own); every modifier
  before it is the dialog's, so `.frame()` and `.padding()` are its width and
  `contentPadding`, never a box around it. `.control(control)` ends it the
  same way for a dialog Bloom's control opens. `.detached()` floats a bottom
  dialog above the safe area with gutters (Bloom's `detached`), and
  `.panelFrame({ maxWidth: '540' })` is the side or bottom panel's own frame
  (Bloom's `panelStyle`); `.frame()` stays the centred card's.

```ts
const PublishAs = view(({ open, onClose }: { open: boolean; onClose: () => void }) =>
  Dialog(AccountRows()).title(t('publishAs')).presented([open, () => onClose()]),
);
```
- **Toast.** A toast is an event, not a view: `toast`, `toast.success`
  `.error` `.warning` `.info` `.loading`, `.promise`, `.dismiss` and
  `.wiggle` are Bloom's own calls, made from a press or an effect, and Bloom's
  toaster (mounted by the app's shell) draws them. A title is handed to Bloom
  as it is; a language view becomes the whole row, as Bloom's custom toast,
  and `toast.custom(view)` is Bloom's `toast.custom`.
- **ConnectionStatus.** Bloom's offline, reconnecting and restored toasts:
  Bloom's localized words and timings, or `.offlineMessage()`,
  `.reconnectingMessage()`, `.restoredMessage()`, `.reconnectingDelay(duration)`
  and `.restoredDuration(duration)` (duration tokens; Bloom's waits are `'3000'`
  and `'2500'`). It draws nothing in place: place it once, near the app's
  root.

Parity (`parity/overlays.cases.tsx`) covers every alert, dialog placement and
tier, and popover side, open and closed, a presented dialog alone and in a
stack, and toasts. Native compares the
committed trees; the web also compares what a case portals into the document's
body, so an open overlay's DOM and CSS and a toast's row are held equal. The
Chrome check compares each cell in place: the presenting view (an overlay's
portal is outside the cells). Toasts are compared by the Jest suites only: a
page has one toast store, so two toasters side by side would each draw both.

## App chrome

The chrome around an app's pages, each the Bloom component an app draws
today: the language node hands it the same props, so it looks and behaves
the same, and `parity/` proves it (see [Component parity](#component-parity)).

### Screen and ScrollView

```ts
export default screen(function Feed() {
  const { posts, more, refreshing, setRefreshing } = useFeed();
  return ScrollView(posts.map((post) => PostRow({ post }).id(post.id)))
    .contentMargins('horizontal', 'lg')
    .spacing('sm')
    .refreshable([refreshing, setRefreshing])
    .onEndReached(more)
    .keyboardTaps('handled')
    .title(t('feed.title'))
    .scrollEdge('auto')
    .screenScroll('content')
    .floatingAction(FAB(Icon(RiQuillPenLine)).label(t('compose')).onPress(compose));
});
```

**Insets on any scroll.** `.safeAreaInset(edge, view, spacing?)` is SwiftUI's
`safeAreaInset`, on a `ScrollView`, a `ScrollArea`, a `ScrollSurface`, a
`List` or a `VirtualList`: the
view is pinned at the scroll's `top` or `bottom`, measured, and the content
gets that much room at the edge (and the spacing), so it scrolls clear of it
(a filter bar over a list, a mini-player above the bar). `.safeAreaBar(edge,
view)` is SwiftUI's `safeAreaBar`: the same, with Bloom's scroll edge scrim
(`EdgeScrim`, the header's own ramp) under the bar, so the content fades as it
passes beneath, as Apple asks of a custom bar over scrolling content.

```ts
ScrollView(...messages).safeAreaBar('bottom', Composer())
ScrollArea(...rows).safeAreaInset('top', FilterChips(), 'sm')
```

It is one mechanism on every platform: a box the scroll fills, the view
absolute at its edge with its height from its own layout, the room added to
the content container's padding over its `contentMargins`. Insets at one edge
stack outward in the order chained. What the content reads of the safe area
(react-native-safe-area-context's `SafeAreaInsetsContext`) is raised by the
same room, as SwiftUI raises the safe area of what is under an inset, so a view
inside that keeps clear of the device's edges keeps clear of the pinned views
too. A `VirtualList`'s room is its `contentContainerStyle`'s padding, and a
`List`'s its scroller's (Bloom's `List` takes `contentContainerStyle`, read
when it scrolls: `.scrollable()`, a height or a section index).

`ScrollView(...children)` is Bloom's `ScreenScrollView`, the screen's
scroller: it binds the header's scroll-edge fade, the bottom bar's fold, the
screen's reselect and, under a footer that follows the keyboard, keeps the
focused input above it. Its children are its content, in a column; it must be
inside a screen, as Bloom's is.

| Modifier | Bloom `ScreenScrollView` |
| --- | --- |
| `.contentMargins(edge?, space)`, `.spacing(space)` | `contentContainerStyle`'s padding (a later margin of an edge replaces the earlier) and `gap` |
| `.contentGrows(on?)` | `contentContainerStyle`'s `flexGrow: 1`: the content container grows to fill the scroll, so content shorter than the viewport (an empty state) centres or sits at its end; `on` a condition (`items.length === 0`) grows it only while it holds. A `ScrollArea`, a `ScrollSurface` and a `VirtualList` take it too |
| `.refreshable([refreshing, setRefreshing], tint?)`, `.refreshable({ refreshing, onRefresh }, tint?)` | a `RefreshControl`: `refreshing` the binding's value, a pull writing `true` (`onRefresh`); the app writes `false` when it has refreshed. Or a refreshing value and the action a pull runs (a query's `isRefetching` and `refetch`), passed as they are. `tint`, a colour role (or a role at an opacity), is its `tintColor` and `colors={[…]}`, painted from the theme as an app writes `tintColor={theme.colors.primary} colors={[theme.colors.primary]}`; `{ ios, android }` is each platform's own (`{ ios: 'primary' }` is `tintColor` alone: Android's spinner keeps its look) |
| `.onEndReached(action, threshold?)` | `onEndReached`, `onEndReachedThreshold` (2 viewports by default); `action` chosen at render, `undefined` included (`hasMore ? loadMore : undefined`), as the app wrote it: `undefined` calls nothing |
| `.scrollIndicators('automatic' \| 'visible' \| 'hidden', axis?)` | `showsVerticalScrollIndicator` and `showsHorizontalScrollIndicator`, or only the one `axis` (`'vertical'` or `'horizontal'`) names: `showsVerticalScrollIndicator={false}` alone is `.scrollIndicators('hidden', 'vertical')` |
| `.scrollDismissesKeyboard('never' \| 'immediately' \| 'interactively')` | `keyboardDismissMode` `none`, `on-drag`, `interactive` |
| `.safeAreaInset(edge, view, spacing?)`, `.safeAreaBar(edge, view)` | a view pinned at the `top` or `bottom` and the content padded by its height (see below) |
| `.keyboardTaps('never' \| 'always' \| 'handled')` | `keyboardShouldPersistTaps` |
| `.stickyIndices(indices)` | `stickyHeaderIndices`: the children, by index, that stay at the top once scrolled to it (a profile's tabs); `undefined` keeps none |
| `.contentView((v) => v…)` | Bloom's `ScreenScrollView contentView` (what an app's `contentContainerClassName` drew): the content laid out in a view of its own inside the content container, which then grows to the viewport and pads only the screen's chrome (a content-sized screen reads no intrinsic height from it). `v` takes the layout of one view: `padding`, `spacing`, `align`, `justify`, `wrap`, `axis`, `frame`, `flex`, `grow`, `shrink`, `basis`, `alignSelf` (the spec's `contentView.modifiers`), drawn as a vertical stack of them is, in layer order (a frame after its padding: one view); the content margins and spacing go on that view after its own, as an app's `contentContainerStyle` followed its class |

Only what the chain names reaches the scroll view, so Bloom's own defaults
hold (`keyboardShouldPersistTaps="handled"` among them). Box modifiers
(`padding`, `frame`, `background`) are a view around the scroller, as
SwiftUI's are; room inside the scroll is `contentMargins`.

A `screen()` body's root takes, besides the header, footer and sheet
modifiers above (see [Navigation](#navigation)), the rest of Bloom `Screen`:

| Modifier | Bloom `Screen` |
| --- | --- |
| `.screenKind('document' \| 'form' \| 'collection' \| 'table' \| 'canvas')` | `kind` (a collection or table scrolls by its list, a canvas not at all) |
| `.screenScroll('auto' \| 'content' \| 'none')` | `scroll`: the screen's viewport, the list in it, or none |
| `.documentScroll()` | `documentScroll`: on the web the window scrolls the page |
| `.headerPlacement('inset' \| 'overlay')` | `headerPlacement` |
| `.scrollEdge('auto' \| 'always' \| 'none')` | the composed header's `scrim` |
| `.underlay(view)` | `underlay`, behind header, content and footer |
| `.floatingAction(view, { placement, visibility })` | `primaryAction`, `primaryActionPlacement`, `primaryActionVisibility` |
| `.contentClearance(space)` | `contentClearance` |
| `.navigationScope('inherit' \| 'shared' \| 'isolated')` | `navigationScope` |
| `.colorPreset(name)` | `colorPreset`: one of Bloom's presets (`AppColorName`), the screen's own recipe for its content, panel and shell, restored when it leaves |
| `.seed('#rrggbb')` | `seed`: the colours from one RGB seed (a colour the person chose), over the preset |
| `.screenActive(false)` | `active={false}`: a retained screen drives no scroll, chrome or reselect |
| `.screenBackground(color)` | `style={{ backgroundColor }}`: the screen's own fill behind its header and content, the surface its chrome is painted for (a reel's or a camera's black); a role is the theme's colour (`useTheme().colors`), an app colour as written |
| `.reselectTop(isAtTop, scrollToTop)` | `reselect`'s `isAtTop` and `scrollToTop`, beside `.onReselect()`'s `refresh`: where the top is for a screen that drives its own scroll (a reel, whose top is its first slide); without it the scroller in front answers |
| `.initialHeaderHeight(space)`, `.initialFooterHeight(space)` | `headerHeight`, `bottomBarHeight`: the chrome's heights before it measures itself (safe area included) |

Three more draw around the `Screen`, as a route's JSX wrapped it:

| Modifier | Around Bloom `Screen` |
| --- | --- |
| `.colorScope('light' \| 'dark' \| undefined)` | `<BloomColorScope mode asChild>`: the screen in a scheme of its own (a dark reel or camera in a light app); `undefined` is the app's, the scope still drawn so a scheme chosen at render never remounts the screen |
| `.keyboardAvoiding(fill?)` | React Native's `<KeyboardAvoidingView>`: filling (React Native's `flex: 1`, or a flex token as `.flex()` writes it: `'1'` is Tailwind's `flex-1`, `flex: 1 1 0%`), padded on iOS and resized elsewhere, as React Native recommends (SwiftUI avoids the keyboard itself; React Native does not) |
| `.provider(Component)` | a provider of children around the screen (Bloom's `<HeaderDockProvider>`, or an app's own): a context the screen's header and content share |

Each wraps what the root's modifiers before it drew, in chain order, as
SwiftUI's modifiers do, so the last is outermost:
`.colorScope('dark').provider(HeaderDock)` is the dock around the scope around
the Screen. The header on iOS (the system bar) and the sheets stay beside the
Screen, as they do without them.

The insets are Bloom `Screen`'s own: the header and the footer measure
themselves, the safe area comes from the screen's context, and the content
pads its ends clear of both. The keyboard is the footer's
(`.footer(content, { keyboard })`: `hide`, `visible`, or `follow`, which rides
the keyboard and keeps the focused field above it) and the scroll view's
(`scrollDismissesKeyboard`, `keyboardTaps`).

A screen of several views returns them in a `Group(...views)`, as Bloom's
`Screen` takes several children: the group draws no view of its own (React's
fragment, SwiftUI's `Group`), so each view is one of the Screen's children, laid
out as the Screen lays them out, and the group's screen modifiers are the
screen's chrome. A group takes only those (and `.id()`): it has no box to pad
or paint. Placed anywhere else, its views are its parent's children where the
group is (in a `ZStack` the group is one layer).

```ts
export default screen(function Thread() {
  return Group(Embed(<Seo title={title} />), ScrollView(...messages))
    .title(title)
    .screenScroll('content')
    .footer(Composer(), { placement: 'overlay', keyboard: 'follow' });
});
```

A seed is the one colour a screen takes as a value rather than a token: it
is data (what the person picked), as an image's source is. `screenActive`
is named for the screen, since `.active()` is an action's state.

### Bottom sheet

```ts
screen(function Saved() {
  const [filters, setFilters] = useState(false);
  return SavedList()
    .title(t('saved.title'))
    .sheet(filters, () => BottomSheet(Filters()).detached().footer(Apply(), { behavior: 'viewport' }), {
      onDismiss: () => setFilters(false),
    });
});
```

A `.sheet(open, content, options)` whose content is a `BottomSheet(...)` node
presents Bloom's `BottomSheet`; any other content keeps Bloom's `Dialog` (from
the bottom, centred from `md`). The node is the sheet's own options, as
SwiftUI's presentation modifiers are; placed anywhere else it draws nothing.

| Modifier | Bloom `BottomSheet` |
| --- | --- |
| `.detached()` | `detached` |
| `.dragIndicator('visible' \| 'hidden')` | `showHandle` |
| `.material('surface' \| 'flat')` | `material` |
| `.scrollable(false)`, `.sizing('content' \| 'fill')` | `scrollable`, `sizing` (a body that owns its list) |
| `.interactiveDismissDisabled()` | `enablePanDownToClose={false}` |
| `.onDismissAttempt(fn)` | `onDismissAttempt` (a veto of back, the backdrop, Escape and a drag) |
| `.manualActivation()` | `manualActivation` |
| `.footer(view, { behavior })` | `footer`, `footerBehavior` |
| `.progress(value)`, `.label(text)` | `animatedProgress`, `accessibilityLabel` |

The sheet is held open by `open` as Bloom's own Dialog holds it: it mounts with
its open state, presents and dismisses as `open` changes, keeps its content
while it leaves, and a close the sheet made itself (a drag, the backdrop, back)
calls `onDismiss`, since the host's state still says open. Its drags are Bloom's
gesture layer (`useDrag` on the sheet's translate; docs/motion.mdx), on the
sheet's own springs.

| | Android, OxyOS | iOS (not verified) | Web |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `.sheet()` | Bloom `Dialog`; a `BottomSheet` content Bloom's `BottomSheet` (on the lens window where Bloom has the native lens) | the system sheet (`@expo/ui`'s `BottomSheet`), the content a React view in it | Bloom `Dialog` or `BottomSheet` |

On iOS a `BottomSheet` node's options are the presentation's:
`dragIndicator` the drag indicator, `interactiveDismissDisabled` the same,
`sizing('fill')` the large detent, otherwise the sheet fits its content, and
its footer follows its body. The system sheet has no title bar, so a
`.sheet()`'s `title` names it for assistive technology only. Without
`@expo/ui`, Bloom's sheet. Bloom's sheet has no detents; neither does the
node.

### Sidebar, Rail and PageFooter

```ts
Sidebar(
  NavItem(t('home'), RiHome5Line).id('home').activeIcon(RiHome5Fill).link('/'),
  NavItem(t('search'), RiSearchLine).id('search').shortcut('⌘K'),
  NavItem(t('settings'), RiSettings3Line).id('settings').secondary(),
  TreeSection(
    t('projects'),
    TreeFolder(t('archive'), ...archived.map((p) => TreeItem(p.name).id(p.id))),
    ...projects.map((p) => TreeItem(p.name).id(p.id).icon(RiFileLine).subtitle(p.owner)),
  ).trailing(Button(Icon(RiAddLine)).label(t('new')).onPress(create)),
)
  .selection([current, setCurrent])
  .surface('docked')
  .footer(AccountRow())
```

`Sidebar(...rows)` is Bloom's `Sidebar`. Its rows are the plain data Bloom's
sidebar reads, so they are rows, not views: a `NavItem(label, icon)` is a
`SidebarNavItem` (`.id` its key, the label by default; `.activeIcon`,
`.badge`, `.link` its `href`, `.shortcut`, `.onPress`, `.onLongPress`;
`.secondary()` pins it at the foot, Bloom's `secondaryItems`), and a
`TreeSection(label, ...rows)` is a `SidebarTree`, its `TreeFolder(label,
...items)` folders and `TreeItem(label, ...items)` rows nested to any depth
(`.icon`, `.subtitle` the row's description, `.meta`, `.link`, `.trailing`
its actions, `.disabled`, `.expanded([open, setOpen])` its open state,
`.emptyLabel`). A section also takes `.collapsible`, `.loading` and `.error`.

| Modifier | Bloom `Sidebar` |
| --- | --- |
| `.selection([id, setId])` | `selected` and `selectedTreeItem`: one selection over destinations and tree rows; `onTreeItemPress` and `onNavigate` (a linking destination; it then takes over the link's push) write it |
| `.header(view \| false)`, `.toolbar(...)`, `.footer(view)` | `header`, `toolbar`, `footer` |
| `.variant('panel' \| 'rail')`, `.surface('card' \| 'plain' \| 'docked')`, `.size('sm' \| 'md' \| 'lg')` | `variant`, `surface`, `size` |
| `.collapsed([collapsed, setCollapsed])` | `collapsed`, `onCollapsedChange` |
| `.searchable(false)`, `.themeToggle(false)` | `showSearch`, `showThemeToggle` |
| `.logo(logo)`, `.account(account)`, `.label(text)` | `logo`, `account`, `accessibilityLabel` |
| `.mode([key, setKey])`, `.modePresentation('segmented' \| 'toggle')`, `.modesLabel(text)` | `mode`, `onModeChange`, `modesPresentation`, `modesLabel`: the switcher of its `SidebarMode` rows |
| `.team(team)`, `.teamPlacement('header' \| 'footer')`, `.plan(plan)` | `team`, `teamPlacement`, `plan` (Bloom's `SidebarTeam` and `SidebarPlan` data) |
| `.onboarding({ title, leading, steps, … })` | `onboarding`: the getting-started card, its `leading` a view |
| `.query([text, onChange])` | `searchQuery`, `onSearchQueryChange`: the quick search held by the app, which filters its own content with it |
| `.onDrop(drop => …)` | `onTreeItemDrop`, told by id: `{ id, from, target?, position, section }` |

A `SidebarMode(label, icon)` row is a mode of the switcher under the header
(`.id` its key, the label by default; `.shortcut`, bound by the sidebar on the
web). A tree row takes `.draggable()`, `.droppable(false)` and
`.dropBeside(false)`, a section `.droppable(false)`: Bloom's drop rules. A
draggable row is an HTML drag source on the web; on touch a long press lifts it
on Bloom's gesture layer (`useDrag` on the row's lift, as the bottom sheet's
drags), and it follows the finger; the moves are accessibility actions too.
The sidebar reports where a row landed; the app moves it.

`.selection` is the one selection for destinations and tree rows, a binding:
Bloom's `selected` and `selectedTreeItem` read it, and its `onTreeItemPress`
and its `onNavigate` write it; `onNavigate`, given, takes over a linking
destination's own push (on the web the browser's navigation is prevented, so
the app's router opens it). A row's `navigate()` press goes through the
navigator of the `layout()` or `screen()` it is drawn under. What a chain
