## Component parity

Every node that stands for a Bloom component is proven against it:
`packages/bloom-language/parity` renders each case twice, the language node
and the Bloom element an app draws today, under Bloom's real provider, in light
and dark (`bun run test:parity`; `bun run test` runs it too).

- **Native:** Bloom's own test environment (its React Native and native-module
  mocks from the repository root), each module resolved as Metro resolves it
  for Android (its `.android`, then its `.native`, then its default file), with
  the portal host an app root mounts, so a modal's native portal draws its
  tree. The two committed host trees must be equal: every host's type, every
  prop (styles flattened, handlers by presence) and its children.
- **Web:** react-native-web in jsdom. Each element is compared on its tag, its
  attributes other than `class` and `style`, its text, and the CSS that
  reaches it: every matching rule of every sheet (the language's adopted one
  included) and its inline style, applied in order, variables resolved, one
  value per longhand.
- **In a browser:** the `Language/Parity` stories put each case's two sides in
  a row, and `scripts/verify-parity-web.mjs` compares every pair in Chrome:
  tags, attributes, text, every computed style property and the laid-out size.
  A case that presents an overlay (a sheet, a palette, the settings modal) or
  pins to the document is `isolated`: each side is drawn alone in a page of
  the same viewport (`Language/Parity frame`) and compared at rest, with what
  it presents into Bloom's portal root.

The cases are one list per family (`parity/<family>.cases.tsx`), which the
three read. A case that cannot be equal is a decision, never a tolerance. A
case whose tree is empty fails, since two empty trees prove nothing, unless
drawing nothing is its claim on that platform (`drawsNothing`: a closed
palette on the web).

Every case runs in the Jest suites, native and web. Every case is in the
`Language/Parity` stories too, except those Chrome cannot compare as a pair
(`*_MOVING`, `*_FOCUSED`, `*_TIMED` and `*_TOASTS` lists beside each family's
cases, which the stories leave out). Each is a property of the page, not of
the case:

| Left out of Chrome | Why two sides cannot be compared in one page | Cases |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A mark that moves on its own | Its frames run on a JavaScript timer or a frame loop from the moment it mounts, so two sides read one after the other stand at different frames. Jest compares them as they mount, where time stands still. | the thinking marks and the working row (agents), the typing dots (chat, message bubbles), a working container's seconds (AI chat), the editor's emotion ring (agent editor), the ambient photo's cross-fade and drift, a calendar's pulsing day, a call's speaking ring and slide track, a busy action's mark (engagement), the locating puck (map chrome), the task list's and web search's working rows |
| A field that takes the focus as it appears | A page has one focused element, so of two sides drawn side by side only one holds it. | an `autoFocus` field and an open collapsible search (inputs), a one-time code, an open home-search location segment |
| A notification that dismisses itself | Its countdown runs as it is drawn, so two sides are sampled at different moments of it. | the timed notifications |
| A toast | A page has one toast store, so two toasters side by side would each draw both toasts. | toasts raised through the language's `toast` and Bloom's |

The Jest web suite cannot compare what needs a layout: jsdom lays nothing
out, so a view that draws only once it has measured itself (the shell's
panels, drawn after the shell knows its width) is compared in Chrome. An
overlay a case presents (an open alert, dialog, popover, picker, tooltip,
hover card, context menu, menubar menu, gallery, attachment menu, the
checkout sheet) is `isolated`, so Chrome compares what it portals as well as
its trigger, as the web suite does. The multi-agent chat is the one
exception: what it presents holds the agents' turning rings, a mark that
moves on its own, so Chrome compares its own view and the web suite its
portal too.

## One spec

`packages/bloom-language/spec/language.json` is the language as data: token
scales, the type ramp, colour roles (theme colour and CSS variable), glass
materials, layering, the environment, every modifier's parameters, and which
node kinds take which modifiers. `bun run generate` (in the package) writes:

- `src/generated/spec.generated.ts`: the runtime's tables;
- `src/generated/web-sheet.generated.ts`: the web stylesheet and its class table;
- `../bloom-language-compiler/crates/compiler/src/spec.generated.rs`: the
  compiler's tables.

Never edit those. A test fails when they are stale, another when a rung differs
from Bloom's, and a type-level test when a node's TypeScript modifiers differ
from the spec's list for its kind.

## Compiler

`packages/bloom-language-compiler` is a Rust pass (Oxc, through a Node addon)
that runs in front of the host's own transformer: a Metro `transformerPath`
wrapper (`withBloomLanguage(config)` from `@oxy.so/bloom-language-compiler/metro`)
and a Jest transformer (`/jest`). It is an optimisation: the runtime interprets
every chain, and the pass changes only what it proves keeps its meaning.

- **Hoists static chains.** A chain whose inputs are literals, imports and
  static module constants (a literal, a chain, a frozen `defineTokens` table)
  is the same immutable node on every call, so it is declared once at module
  scope, after the last declaration it reads. A dynamic chain keeps its runtime
  parts and hoists what is static inside it, including its longest static
  prefix (`HStack(…).padding('md')` before `.spacing(gap)`). Names resolve
  through scope analysis, so a local that shadows an import is never hoisted.
- **Adds `"use memo"`** to every function passed to `view()`.
- **Builds a root as it renders.** A dynamic `toElement(chain)` in JSX becomes
  `<LanguageRoot build={() => chain} />`, so the handlers and refs the chain
  takes reach the React Compiler as one JSX prop, never as arguments a call
  might read during render (which costs the whole component its memoization).
  A bare `view()` node stays `toElement`, which mounts its component, and so
  does a chain of host views that reads nothing and takes no handler or ref
  (the spec's `reads`), which `toElement` draws as those views, with no
  boundary to build it in.
- **Checks chains** against the spec: an unknown modifier for the node, a wrong
  argument count, or a literal token outside its set fails the build with the
  source line and a suggestion. Every chain is checked before anything is
  hoisted, including the chains nested in a hoisted one.

It does not resolve tokens: that stays the runtime's, so the two cannot disagree
about what a token means, and its output is the same program on every platform.

**Parity is the gate.** CI renders every fixture in
`packages/bloom-language-compiler/fixtures` interpreted and compiled, on native
and web, and compares the committed trees on mount, after a re-render, and after
a theme switch; each fixture also pins how many chains the compiler hoists.

**Distribution.** Installing the compiler needs no Rust. Its platform
packages (`@oxy.so/bloom-language-compiler-<platform>`, its optional
dependencies) are committed manifests under `npm/`, written by
`bun run generate:platforms` (napi-rs) and held to it by
`test/platform-packages.test.mjs`; run it after a version bump. CI (the
`Language compiler binaries` workflow) builds the addon with napi-rs for linux
x64 and arm64 (glibc), darwin x64 and arm64 and win32 x64, and a
`wasm32-wasip1-threads` build; packs each into its platform package
(`scripts/pack-prebuilt.mjs`); and installs the packs on each platform with no
cargo on PATH, where the addon and the WASI build compile every fixture alike
(`scripts/verify-prebuilt.cjs`). The loader (`js/native.js`) takes a build from
the checkout (`bun run build:addon`), else the platform's package, else the
WASI build (a musl Linux, or a platform with no addon). The release
(`release-packages.yml`) runs that workflow when the compiler's version is not
on npm yet, stages each addon in its platform package
(`scripts/stage-prebuilt.mjs`) and hands them to Oxy's shared release, which
publishes the platform packages before the compiler.

## Built and not built

The whole language is built. One line holds it (`feat/bloom-language-final`):
the core, spec and compiler with its distribution; the platform components and
navigation; the primitives, numeric tokens, layout and style modifiers and
variants; the motion engine; the SwiftUI adoptions; the className codemod and
the rules that move Bloom JSX; Bloom without NativeWind; and
`@oxy.so/bloom-data` beside it. Each area below says what is built and, after
**Decided**, what the language does not do on purpose, each a design decision
with its reason. Nothing else is missing.

- **Core.** The node model, `view()`, the one spec (runtime tables, web sheet
  and the compiler's tables from `spec/language.json`) and the compiler
  (checks, hoisting), with unit tests, cargo tests and parity against
  hand-written Bloom on native and web, light and dark; the compiler's
  prebuilt addons per platform and its WASI fallback, built and packed by CI
  so installing it needs no Rust (see [Compiler](#compiler)).
- **Renderers.** On a device the nodes are React Native's native views, and
  where a platform has a component of its own the language draws that one:
  on iOS the system menu (SwiftUI `Menu` through `@expo/ui`), Liquid Glass
  (`expo-glass-effect`) under a glass, a Button's and FAB's glass and the
  bottom bar's capsule, the navigation bar and toolbar (expo-router's native
  `Stack`), the system tab bar (`NativeTabs`), the system sheet and SF
  Symbols in those; on Android and OxyOS the lens (`@oxy.so/bloom-lens`) for
  the menu, a glass, a Button's and FAB's glass, a floating header's controls
  and the bottom bar's capsule; on the web react-native-web, the language's
  generated stylesheet and Bloom's web components (see
  [Platform components](#platform-components)). A server-rendered page carries
  the sheets (`LanguageStyles`), the ink sheets of colours at an opacity
  included. Neither Bloom nor the language uses NativeWind, react-native-css
  or Tailwind: a style is Bloom's tokens and the language's generated sheet,
  and `hoverGroup()` spreads the marker Bloom's `SubtleHover` reads
  (docs/no-nativewind.mdx). Verified: Android on a Pixel, the web in Chrome;
  iOS is typechecked and tested against stand-ins. **Decided:** there is no
  renderer of the language's own beside React Native (no SwiftUI or CSS
  output of a whole tree): Bloom's components are the look, and the platform
  components above are drawn where they exist.
- **Primitives.** Stacks, `ZStack` (sized to its largest child, each placed at
  its `.alignment()`, SwiftUI's `ZStack(alignment:)`), `.overlay(view,
  alignment)` and `.background(view, alignment)`, `Spacer`, `Text` (Bloom's
  `Text`, its families and ramp, mono), `Icon`, `Image`, `Embed`, `Avatar`
  and `AvatarGroup`, `Badge`, `IconCircle`, `Loading`, `Skeleton`, `Shape`,
  `Divider`, `Fill`, `AspectRatio`, `Grid`, `Button`, `ButtonGroup`, `FAB`,
  `Lottie`, the action bar. Bloom's helpers and constants that draw nothing
  (`computeMortgage`, `listingQualityScore`…) are plain functions called in a
  view body (see [Interop](#interop)). **Decided:** a `ZStack` sizes itself
  from its children's reported heights, since React Native's layout cannot
  size a box by the largest of children drawn over each other; until they
  report (one layout pass) they sit in their slots one under another.
- **Tokens.** Bloom's named rungs; numeric tokens by one rule (an exact value
  apps write, named by its value), durations included (`'0'`, `'250'`,
  `'400'`, `'800'`, `'1000'`, `'2500'`, `'3000'`, `'4000'`) and every
  component's own waits (a hover card's and a tooltip's delays, a
  notification's dismissal and intro, a connection status's timings, an
  auto-hiding bar's peeks); a reveal's timing a timed motion token (an
  accordion's, a collapsible's, a meter's and a rating bar's); a
  signature's stroke a border width; a card's corner as a corner token of
  Bloom's radius scale; a node's densities its own; and an app's own tokens
  (`defineTokens`: space, icon and avatar sizes, leading, motion, and brand
  colours). **Decided:** a motion is a duration and a curve, so it is named
  (Bloom's motion table or an app's `defineTokens({ motion })`), never by one
  number; Bloom's default hide and show of an auto-hiding bar are its own
  when no motion is chained, since they are not on Bloom's motion scale.
- **Bindings.** A node's main value is its constructor's binding, and every
  other value it edits a modifier taking a `Binding` (`.presented()`,
  `.query()`, `.expanded()`, `.sorting()`, `.refreshable()`…), across every
  family, the selections a press reports included (a bottom bar's, a
  sidebar's, a rail's and the settings' `.selection([id, setId])`, an agent
  chat's, a chart's active tile) and the values inside
  Bloom's data that an app edits (a mail row's `.starred()`, a cart's
  `.tip()` and `.promo()`, a call's toggles, a map's `.tilted()`).
  **Decided:** an event that holds no value stays an event: a one-off
  choice (a suggestion, an emoji, a reaction a second press removes, a
  date picked from a calendar), a press that opens something, a jump the
  app performs (an outline's heading), an action on the app's data (select
  all, mark unread), and a report of derived state nothing writes back (a
  card form's scheme, a loading avatar); a value written by something other
  than the person (the playing track, the read position) is a plain value; a
  value per entry of a list Bloom owns (a list's mail entries, a cart line's
  quantity) stays in its entry, since a binding is one value; a value a
  component keeps itself and reports (a chart card's `default*` with its
  `on*Change`) stays the component's, as Bloom keeps it; a flow the app
  steps through (the checkout sheet's step and draft) stays the app's state
  machine.
- **Layout and style.** Distribution, placement, overlays, elevation, rings,
  corners and their curve (`'infinite'` is Tailwind's `rounded-full`, and a
  corner or edge may be physical: left, right, top-left…), margins and
  `flex()`, `childSpacing()` (Tailwind's `space-x`/`space-y`), hit testing
  (`true`, `false`, `'children'`, `'self'`), text styles (`textTertiary`
  among the roles, tabular figures, `textAlign('left' | 'right')`), links,
  truncation, and `RawText` (React Native's own Text).
  On native a class's fixed colour at an opacity, a leading and a tracking
  are drawn as NativeWind's compiler rounded them, and on the web a colour
  at an opacity is an ink (`var(--bl-…)`) a variant can override. An app
  colour is a hex, an `rgb()`/`rgba()` of numbers or a CSS colour name both
  platforms paint alike. **Decided:** none.
- **Variants.** Breakpoints, the scheme, a view's own hover, focus
  (`:focus-visible`) and press, `disabled`, the platforms and an app's own
  boolean (`.when(isOpen, …)`); nested
  variants; an app's colours and other app tokens (on the web the view's
  style picked in JS, as a Bloom component's); a material's padding, frame,
  border, corner and fill (a glass, an island, a concentric box), a Button's
  or FAB's glass with the control's own state; geometry (`.scale()`,
  `.offset()`, `.rotation()`) by component, a custom property per
  component on the web, played by `.animation()`; on the web a sheet per
  condition holding only the classes that render; a Bloom component's
  variants in its own style, its state tracked by its box; the innermost
  pressable owns a hover or a press, as react-native-web's does, a Bloom
  component's or an `Embed`'s included. **Decided:** a material is never
  faded (an opacity on a material or its ancestor takes its backdrop blur
  away on the web, AGENTS.rules.md, Material); a glass's tint is a colour
  role, at rest and in a variant (the platforms' glasses take one); a
  concentric box's corner is its parent's less the inset, so it has none to
  vary; a glass and an island clip to one radius, at rest and in a variant
  (Bloom's `Surface` and `BlurIsland` take one), so a corner of some sides
  only is left out on them; an `.anchor()` is one point per view (a variant changes how far a
  view turns, not about what); geometry a `MotionValue`, a gesture or press
  feedback moves takes no variant (the one mapper writes it).
- **Glass and platform components.** Per platform: Menu, Button, Header,
  BottomBar, FAB, the glass (the lens on Android and OxyOS, `glassEffect` on
  iOS, Bloom's material on the web), an island's controls on its shared
  material, and navigation (`AppLayout`, stacks, routes, tabs, the iOS system
  components). **Decided:** on iPad the sidebar is Bloom's `AppShell`
  sidebar; an iOS sheet's title is an accessibility name (the system sheet
  has no title bar).
- **From SwiftUI** (each with its spec, compiler checks and docs above): glass
  groups (`GlassGroup`, `.glassID()`, `.glassTransition()`, `.glassUnion()`),
  `interactive` and `identity` glass, the environment (`.disabled()`,
  `.controlSize()`, `.buttonStyle()`, `defineEnvironment`), the glass button
  styles, `confirmationDialog`, custom modifiers, `.safeAreaInset()` and
  `.safeAreaBar()`, `ToolbarGroup` and `ToolbarSpacer`, `.phases()` and
  keyframe tracks, `ViewThatFits`, and the Liquid Glass rules in the compiler.
  **Decided** (see Glass groups): on Android a group draws each glass as its
  own shape, blended, united and moved by id in the lens engine; a union is
  the platform's merge; the web has no shared sampling;
  on iOS only a group of glass icon buttons morphs by SwiftUI's
  `glassEffectID`, since those are the one glass the language draws as
  SwiftUI controls, and any other glass is a React Native view under
  `expo-glass-effect`, which has no identity to morph by; the compiler checks
  the Liquid Glass rules within the one expression that builds a group, since
  following views across a `view()`'s boundary is a type checker's work, not
  a chain compiler's (the runtime draws what it is given).
- **Motion** (`@oxy.so/bloom/motion`). Motion tokens and curves,
  `.animation()` (geometry and opacity; its scope reaches the `view()` bodies
  inside it), `.animatesColors()` (colours, opt-in: `transition-colors`; a
  theme switch never plays), `.transition()`, `.keyframes()`, loops
  (`.repeatForever('spin' | 'pulse')`, Tailwind's `animate-spin` and
  `animate-pulse`, or an app's keyframes), `.delay()` (duration tokens),
  `.anchor()` (a transform origin), `.matchedGeometry()` within one tree and
  between screens (a `transitionNamespace()`: Bloom's own flight over every
  screen, on native and the web), `.contentTransition('roll')` and
  `.contentTransition('numeric')` (each character that changed rolls),
  `.pressScale()`, geometry (`.scale()`, `.offset()`, `.rotation()`) as
  tokens or bound to a `MotionValue`, `useMappedValue()` (a value that
  follows another through a range), `.drag()`, `.swipe()`, `.pinch()` and
  `.onDoubleTap()` on Gesture Handler 3. Resting geometry that nothing plays
  is the box's own style. Bloom's pop, roll, toast and press feedback play
  on the same engine; the bottom sheet's drags are the same gesture layer's
  (docs/motion.mdx, Motion values and gestures). **Decided:** the language
  animates by declaration (`.animation(motion, value)` on the view whose
  change plays), not by a transaction around a state write (`withAnimation`):
  React batches and schedules renders apart from the write, so a transaction
  would have to guess which render it belongs to; Tailwind's
  `animate-bounce` and `animate-ping` are not loop tokens, since no app writes
  them (an app loops its own keyframes); `transition-all` is
  `.animation(m).animatesColors()`, and a frame's size and a shadow change at
  once, since the engine moves and paints boxes and does not lay them out or
  redraw shadows per frame; a colour plays on the view's moving box (its
  outermost), so an inner layer's fill and a native `Text`'s ink change at
  once (each would need an animated host of its own; chain the fill
  outermost); a conditional child (`show && view`) leaves at once, while a
  keyed child plays its removal (`[show && view.id('x')]`), since tracking
  every child would put a `Presence` around every child of every stack;
  matched geometry between screens is Bloom's own flight, not Reanimated's
  `sharedTransitionTag`, which needs an app-level flag, works only between
  native-stack screens and not on the web.
- **className.** The className table (`docs/bloom-language-classnames.mdx`)
  says what each utility the apps use becomes; the codemod
  (`packages/bloom-language-codemod`, docs/bloom-language-codemod.mdx) moves
  an app's `className` and Bloom JSX to the language where it renders the
  same, by a rule per Bloom component (a route's `PageHeader` as an in-page
  `Header`, presented dialogs, a `Screen`'s several views as a `Group`, a
  `VirtualList`'s rows and modifiers, a scroll's content class as its
  `.contentView()`…), proven by its parity corpus. Bloom itself has no
  NativeWind (docs/no-nativewind.mdx). **Decided:** a utility the table
  flags is not moved until it is a modifier or a named token: a raw value
  never becomes a number in the language; a difference the parity measures
  stays JSX with its reason.
- **Inputs** (see [Inputs](#inputs)). `TextField`, `SecureField`,
  `TextEditor`, `Field`, `Label`, `Toggle`, `Checkbox`, `RadioGroup`
  (swatches included), `Segmented`, `Picker` with their `Option` and
  `OptionGroup`, and `Search`, each value a `Binding`, focus included
  (`.focused(binding)`); a trailing field icon, a completion, a picker row's
  mark, a search's trigger, trailing content and box.
- **Overlays and feedback** (see [Overlays and feedback](#overlays-and-feedback)).
  `.alert()`, `.dialog()` and `.popover()` on any view, each shown while its
  binding holds (Bloom's `AlertDialog`, `Dialog` and `Popover`, the view as
  the popover's trigger), with an alert's icon, a dialog's header, footer,
  page, size, padding and every tier's placement, and a popover's offsets
  and sizes; a dialog as a view of its own (`Dialog(...).presented(binding)`);
  `ConnectionStatus` with its own words and timings; `toast` as
  Bloom's imperative call with a title or a language view, and
  `toast.custom`. **Decided:** a `.sheet()` whose content is not a
  `BottomSheet` is Bloom's `Dialog`; Bloom's sheet has no detents, so neither
  does the node.
- **Containers** (see [Containers](#containers)). `Card` and its parts, `Row`
  (Bloom's `Item`), `GroupedSection`, `GroupedSectionItem` (with a box of its
  own), `GroupedSectionIcon`, `GroupedSectionDivider` and
  `GroupedSectionCollapse`, `Chip` (a filter chip through a binding, its
  role, the inverted chip), `ChipRow` and `CappedChipRow`, `EmptyState` (its
  actions and suggestions read from `Button` nodes, a `navigate()`
  included), `Admonition`, `Tabs`/`TabItem`/`TabContent` (the selection a
  binding, reordering), `SelectionCard`, `Kbd` and `ErrorBoundary` (a box, a
  fallback that is a function of the error).
- **App chrome** (see [App chrome](#app-chrome)), each Bloom's own component
  with parity on native, the web and Chrome: `ScrollView` and all of
  `screen()`'s `Screen` (kind, scroll owner, document scroll, header
  placement and scroll edge, underlay, floating action, clearance, navigation
  scope, colour preset and seed, activity, starting chrome heights, several
  views in a `Group`); a
  `.sheet()`'s `BottomSheet` (iOS: the system sheet); `Sidebar` with its
  destinations, tree, modes, team, plan, onboarding card, search query and
  tree drops (a touch drag on Bloom's gesture layer), `Rail`, `PageFooter`;
  `Command`; `SettingsModal` and the settings pages' rows
  (`SettingsSection`, `SettingsValue`, `SettingsSelect`, `SettingsTextField`,
  `SettingsDateField`); `AppLayout`'s semantic layout, panels, deck and
  inspector, and its destinations (Bloom's pager provider); the layout
  family's `Toolbar`, `StickySection`, `ScrollArea`, `ScrollSurface` (its
  fade a colour role), `AutoHideBar` (its band a colour role, its peeks
  duration tokens, its hide and show motion tokens) and `ResizeHandle`.
 
- **Generic components** (see [Generic components](#generic-components)),
  drawn as Bloom's own: the date and time pickers, ratings, meters and
  progress bars, stickers, tables, rows and lists, carousels, markdown,
  sliders, accordions, code, steppers, tooltips, breadcrumbs, activity
  heatmaps, tag fields, file uploads, videos, one-time codes, colour swatch
  pickers, animated checks, context menus, collapsibles, drop zones, media
  inset borders, image aspect ratios, fact strips, photo grids, choice lists,
  category bars, video playback, hover cards, expandable text, menubars,
  composition bars, phone inputs, media flights, avatar flights, input
  groups, notifications, link previews, social buttons, media galleries, data
  tables, sortable chip rows, viewports; and, of wave 7, `ConnectionDots`,
  `OutlineNav`, `Pagination`, `ProgressiveBlur`, `RowActions`, `SwipeRow` on
  Gesture Handler and `ThemeToggle`.
- **Product components**, each Bloom's own with parity on native, web and
  Chrome. Wave 5: `AgentAvatar`; the chat composer family (`ChatComposer`,
  `CompactComposer`, `ComposerBanner`, `ComposerIconButton`,
  `SuggestionList`, `VoiceRecorder`, `AttachmentMenu`, `EmojiPicker`,
  `ReactionPicker`, `MessageContextMenu`); the chart cards (`AreaChartCard`,
  `BarListCard`, `ContributionsCard`, `HeatmapChartCard`, `LineChartCard`,
  `RadarChartCard`, `SleepScoreCard`, `StageBarsCard`, `StepsCard`,
  `TokensChartCard`, `ChartCardSurface`, `ChartHeadline`, `ChartStatTiles`,
  `Sparkline`); the composer panel (`ComposerPanel`, `ComposerPill`,
  `ComposerPanelStatusTab`); the AI chat (`AiChatShell`, `AiChatContainer`,
  `AiChatThread`, `AiChatUserMessage`, `AiChatAssistantMessage`,
  `AiChatMessageLine`, `AiChatHeader`, `AiChatMobileHeader`,
  `AiChatCodePanel`, `AiChatGalleryPanel`); `StatCard`, `StatCards`;
  `PlayButton`, `PlaybackProgress`; the agent families (`AgentLimitsCard`,
  `UsageCard`, `AgentThinking`, `AgentProgress`, `AgentProgressLoadingText`,
  the agent log's five, `ComposerLoader`, `ConversationSidebar`,
  `Questionnaire`); the chat families (the chat screen's, chat list's,
  indicators' and message media's 24); the listing details, map markers,
  follow button, shelves, subscription plans and user hover card; the card
  form and its parts; the checkout sheet; their lists of colours are
  `.gradient()` and their secondary looks modifiers. Wave 6, the families one
  app uses: Homiio's (booking, home search, listing actions as
  `ListingActionBar`, listing cards, the listing editor, the offering badge,
  price insights, the stay filters and search, tenancy, eviction, place
  reviews); GoWay's map chrome, places and directions, and Mercaria's place
  picker; Gemies' and Astro's (the agent editor, the reply's identity, the
  multi-agent chat, the task list, the voice controls, web search, the AI
  profile card, the agent chat); Allo's call screens, people and message
  bubbles; Inbox's mail and note toolbar, and the mail row
  (`@oxy.so/bloom/mail-list` holds its shared helpers, constants, types and
  parts, live code with no component of its own); Nilo's project board and
  activity feed; Peable's order status, Atlas' lockup, Willo's ambient photo
  and the month calendar; and the action bar's orientation, tone and zero
  counts Mention sets. Wave 7, the families no app imports yet: commerce
  (addresses, payment methods, the price summary, payment status, the cart,
  menu items); the dashboard cards
  (`ImportantAlertsCard`, `PatientInfoCard`); `RouteStops`. **Decided:** colours that come
  with an item of the data (a reply preview's inside a message bubble's
  data, a swipe row's `paint`,
  an alert's tone) stay
  in that item, as Bloom takes them, since they belong to the item, not to
  the view; content Bloom takes inside its data (a price line's `info`) stays
  data, not language
  children, for the same reason.
- **Parity.** Every family above is compared against hand-written Bloom in
  the Jest suites, native and web, and in Chrome, light and dark, an
  overlay's portal included (see [Component parity](#component-parity)).
  **Decided:** a case whose look depends on the page rather than on the
  case is compared by Jest and left out of Chrome, where two sides share one
  page: a mark that moves on its own timer (two sides are read at different
  frames), a field that takes the focus as it appears (a page has one
  focus), a notification counting down (sampled at different moments) and a
  toast (a page has one toast store). A view that draws only once it has
  measured itself (the shell's panels) is compared in Chrome, since jsdom
  lays nothing out.
